#6822. Before & After: How an Artist and Architect Couple Turned the “Worst House” on the Block Into Their Dream Home

In Brooklyn, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier transform a crumbling townhouse with equal parts drama and restraint.

Before they stepped foot inside the crumbling brownstone on a landmark block in Brooklyn’s Boerum Hill, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier had toured 30 homes and browsed more than 200 online. Neither of them expected to become instantly captivated by the 1869 Italianate townhouse, which was being sold in a stately yet dilapidated condition, having once served as a single-room occupancy (SRO) residence. But it happened nonetheless.

“This was the first one that had the bones that Katherine wanted,” recalls Gauthier, who runs the firm GAuthier Architects. “It follows the classic real estate rule of buying the worst house on the best block.”

The strategy paid off: Once their offer was accepted, the couple embarked on an 18-month-long odyssey that saw them jump through a series of hoops (preservationists; concerned neighbors; skeptical engineers) to completely transform the moldering pile into a creative haven for them to live, work, and raise their daughter.

A true joint-effort, Gauthier oversaw a modest two-story rear expansion and structural alterations, while Keltner took charge of spatial arrangements as well as color, pattern, texture, and artwork—much of it her own.

Once deteriorating, the interiors now have an easy elegance, with plaster walls, arched passageways, and curved corners that give the soaring ceilings an intimate feel. Pink takes an unexpected starring role, appearing throughout the parlor floor and also in the zellige-clad primary bath, but other adventurous hues like mint green and crimson also feature prominently. Capping it off is a plywood-sheathed office for Gauthier’s practice, allowing the family retreat to double as a place of creative inspiration.

Below, Keltner and Gauthier share their biggest lessons (and some “before” pictures) from the painstaking passion project.

When the townhouse was converted to an SRO, it had been chopped up into at least 13 units and two kitchens, and few of the original decorative elements were still intact. A couple had bought it in the 1960s and made it a family home again and added an art gallery space. But by 2021, when Keltner and Gauthier first saw it, the house was in a state of severe neglect, with peeling paint, crumbling floors, ancient appliances—not to mention a 1,500-pound printing press in the studio (that the couple later preserved). Yet, Keltner and Gauthier saw an opportunity, not to recreate the past, but rather forge their own set of rules.

“The house wasn’t burdened with pristine historic details—our moldings were trashed,” says Gauthier. “There were a few things that we kept and celebrated, but to me, it was like a tabula rasa. We can just go in and make it ours.” Katherine agreed: “I feel like sometimes having to preserve things is more expensive than just starting clean,” she says. “And so I thought that was an advantage for us.”

Not having to be too precious with ornamentation allowed the couple to make a bold statement about crown moldings, eschewing tradition and installing curving corners and coved ceilings instead. Nowhere does the effect have more impact than in the parlor, where Gauthier devised a ceiling light in the shape of a giant oval that casts light upward rather than down. “I really like indirect lighting,” he says.

Crafted out of laser-cut plywood, the elliptical fixture is an abstracted homage to the ceiling medallions found in neighboring historic brownstones. “Some of them are incredibly ornate and beautiful, but they don't really have any use—they’re just decoration,” says Gauthier. “Adding ambient lighting to the living space takes that historical reference in pocket.”

Once they were through the process of changing the structure’s zoning from an SRO to a single-family home, Gauthier and Keltner had to contend with its landmarked status, meaning a series of approvals were required for any additions or exterior modifications.

The couple originally planned to construct a three-story extension in the back, but they ended up compromising on two floors. This expansion includes a bump-out for the parlor floor’s kitchen, leading to a rear deck that steps down to the garden. On top, a private terrace connects to the primary bath. “Instead of having that third story, we now have a deck off of our primary suite,” says Keltner. “It’s kind of amazing,” adds Gauthier.

One decision the couple wrestled with mightily was whether to replace the original, 150-year-old pine flooring with ebony-stained white oak. “The floors were part of the reason we liked the house so much,” says Gauthier. But their contractor talked them out of keeping them. “It’s a lot of extra work for them,” says Keltner. “They have to take it up, save it, put something else down, and then put it back. I think we reached a point in time and money where we were like, Okay, fine.”

Another flooring regret was not adding underfoot heating. “I was pushing the mechanical engineers to do radiant heating and radiant cooling,” says Gauthier. “Radiant heat is really lovely—you have warmth under your feet all the time.” But the mechanics behind the system proved complicated and technically redundant, as the home was already configured with a forced-air HVAC system.

“In doing research and other projects, it’s clear that the cost-to-pleasure ratio would have been worth it,” he says now. “We regret not putting the warm floors in.”

While windows might sometimes be an afterthought, Keltner and Gauthier have advice: Go with triple-glazed. “Windows provide heat and cool insulation as well as security,” explains Keltner. “And they can define both the outside and the inside of a space.” The couple went with triple-glazed windows from Austrian-based firm Bewiso that come pre-wired for easy connection to alarm systems. “They’re almost like hurricane windows,” she says.

“You can't break through three panes of glass,” says Gauthier. Due to the building’s landmark status, they had to use historic-style windows on the front facade. “We were able to get these old-fashioned type of windows that when you close them, they feel like you're closing a Mercedes,” says Gauthier. “They completely silence the outdoors.”

The final piece of advice is perhaps the most crucial: “Hire the best contractor you can afford,” says Gauthier.

“The typical advice is to get a few bids and throw out the lowest and the highest,” says Keltner. “People usually take the second lowest.” However, that could be the determining factor between an on-time project and one that drags. “You’ll end up paying the difference both in money and time,” she says. “It’s often hard for people to bite the bullet and go with the slightly higher price, but it really pays off.”

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6821. The 2026 CFDA Fashion Awards Celebrated Fashion’s Next Generation—and Its Icons

Rachel Scott, Ralph Lauren, and Colleen Allen were among the night’s top honorees.

Often called the Oscars of American fashion, the CFDA Fashion Awards lived up to the billing last night, drawing a starry crowd to the American Museum of Natural History. The ceremony, now in its 45th year, recognized the designers and creatives shaping the direction of American fashion, from established names to independent newcomers.

Before guests headed inside the museum halls, they stepped onto the red carpet to channel the evening’s dress code, “American design excellence,” where many of the evening’s nominees and honorees were well-represented. ELLE cover star Mona Tougaard wore a punchy primary-color look by Zane Li of LII, who was nominated in the American Emerging Designer of the Year category. Demi Moore and Lupita Nyong’o chose to rework American wardrobe classics in custom GapStudio. And Hailey Bieber, Zoë Kravitz, Rosé, and Anja Rubik glimmered in gilded Saint Laurent looks from Anthony Vaccarello’s final collection for the brand. (Vaccarello was honored with the International Designer of the Year Award.)

Then, Thom Browne, the CFDA’s chairman of the board of directors, started the ceremony with powerful opening remarks: “American fashion is at the center of the world and at the center of the spirit of our time,” he said. “It’s never been more important to tell important stories.”

Browne handed the reins off to host Keke Palmer, who delivered a major fashion moment in 1970s Bob Mackie previously worn by Raquel Welch, to begin awarding the evening’s most anticipated prizes. Rachel Scott was crowned American Womenswear Designer of the Year, her second time earning the prize, while Ralph Lauren, set to celebrate 60 years in business in 2027, won American Menswear Designer of the Year.

In her speech, Scott issued a call to action that was especially poignant ahead of the midterm elections. “In a world where independent and marginalized voices are systematically quieted, I feel extremely lucky and very, very responsible,” she said. “And we have a lot of work to do, starting tomorrow morning.”

Colleen Allen, beloved by the New York fashion scene for her bewitchingly feminine but powerful designs, won the coveted Google Shopping American Emerging Designer of the Year Award, while Ana Khouri took home Accessory Designer of the Year for her sculptural jewelry and gravity-defying ear cuffs.

Also included in the programming were a series of special honors, including Tory Burch’s Positive Change Award, Peter Marino’s Fashion Icon Award, and Inez and Vinoodh’s Geoffrey Beene Lifetime Achievement Award.

See the full list of the 2026 CFDA Fashion Award winners below.

#6820. Here’s Where Wilnie and Wilson Stand Now After Breaking Up on ‘Love Is Blind’

If Wilson adjusts his expectations about cooking, that is.

#6819. The Best Dressed Celebrities at the CFDA Awards

All decked out in their favorite American designers

At most awards shows that we cover—the Oscars, the Golden Globes, the Grammys—fashion is a secondary concern for the judges and nominees. At the CFDAs, however, the entire evening is dedicated to the American fashion industry, and, much to our annual delight, the attendees always dress with that in mind. Last night’s wardrobe selections were not dominated by European fashion houses (or celebrity contracts). Instead, guests seized the opportunity to celebrate American talent, much of which is based here in New York, with collections equally worthy of a spot on the red carpet.

Take, for instance, Mona Tougaard in a sweet, colorblocked look from Lii by Zane Li, who was nominated in the emerging category this year. Or actress Sydney Lemmon, who wore a long-sleeved, button-front black sheath worn just undone enough to reveal a pair of red, bedazzled panties underneath, created by Emily Dawn Long, another emerging nominee. And then there was returning nominee—and winner!—Colleen Allen, who outfitted guest Gracie Abrams in a strapless, twisted, black velvet gown.

Those who represented the larger American brands leaned into a more relaxed approach to dressing. Emily Ratajkowski wore a simple black sweater paired with a low-rise orange skirt and black boots from Tory Burch. Elle Fanning showed up in a dusty pink slip beneath a floor-length leather trench from Coach by Stuart Vevers. (Each was nominated for awards: womenswear and accessory designer, respectively.)

And then came Anthony Vaccarello, nominated for International Designer of the Year, swanning in from Europe with his high-glam, gold-foiled posse. Hailey Bieber, Zoë Kravitz, Rosé, and Anja Rubik each wore a look from his megawatt Spring 2027 collection, which was almost entirely blanketed in gold. But they still managed to retain some semblance of casual American ease with bomber jackets layered over knee-length skirts and bedazzled, midriff-baring two-piece sets.

All in all, it was a very well dressed celebration of the country’s fashion industry, so picking our favorites was difficult. But ahead, we’ve rounded up the best-dressed guests of the 2027 CFDA Awards.

#6816. How Her Love of Vintage Changed Writer Keli Goff’s Life

Her collection, which has given new life to some of the great Black designers, is now a museum show.

When Keli Goff began purchasing vintage, it was a way to put together stylish looks for her new job within her budget. But over the years, as her interest grew, collecting became a way of connecting to the stories of Black designers unrecognized during their lifetimes. Goff, a writer who has been on the staff of shows including Being Mary Jane and And Just Like That, now owns work from major fashion figures like Patrick Kelly, Isaia Rankin, Stephen Burrows, and Ann Lowe, together making up one of the biggest private collections of work by Black designers. This month, the Houston Museum of African American Culture presents “Collecting in Color: Highlights from the Keli Goff Collection,” which was curated by John Guess and Danielle Finnerman. It’s the first major exhibition focused on her collection, and some of the pieces will be donated to the Bard Graduate Center’s Study Collection.

Goff’s interest in fashion design has intersected with her writing career in interesting ways. The estate of legendary costume designer Edith Head chose Goff to adapt her story for a biopic. “It’s all so surreal. I obviously have admired Edith’s work as long as I can remember, so my collection bringing this project into my life is a dream come true,” she says. Last year, Goff completed studying to become an art appraiser. She now consults in the art and auction sphere in addition to writing.

“People have been fascinated by my collection,” Goff says. “It’s a bit rare to see, frankly, collectors in this space like me. I mean like me in all sorts of ways, not just as an African American woman, but an African American woman who’s not from a privileged background. It’s important to me that I’m not the last one like me in these spaces.”

Goff spoke to ELLE about the joy of working with a museum in her Texas hometown, the old movies that inspired her love of vintage, and the designers she wishes everyone knew.

It really started by watching old movies with my mom and watching her dress up [when I was] a little girl. When I show friends old photos of her, people are always thinking, “Oh, she looks like a movie star or very Diana Ross-esque.” We would watch old movies, especially Imitation of Life and things from that era, the real glamour, the golden age of Hollywood. She would always say, “Look at the dresses, look at the dresses,” and I would. I really fell in love with old movies, which I do believe is part of how I ended up becoming a writer.

But also, I really fell in love with vintage clothes. I moved to New York and didn’t have a lot of money, and vintage was not trendy and didn’t cost thousands of dollars like it can be today. For one of my earliest jobs, I had to go to fancy events, but I had no money. I bought a couple of pieces from the ’60s at a flea market. I remember being at this event, and all these women who obviously had a lot more money to spend on clothes, asking, “Who’s this by? What boutique did you buy this at?” And I was like, “Oh, I got it at a flea market on Avenue B.” I think they thought I was joking.

I wrote a piece for The Daily Beast several years ago about the most influential Black designers in history. It was one of the most memorable pieces I’ve written. I don’t think at the time I was aware of how profoundly it impacted me, but I learned how many seminal Black American designers died of AIDS in a really short window of time. Willi Smith, one of the first Black designers to achieve major crossover commercial success in department stores, died. And so did Isaia Rankin and then Patrick Kelly. What I took away is that so much history was lost, but I also think about how different the future of American fashion might be today because these were really trailblazing and hugely influential.

They weren’t just influential Black American designers, but hugely influential American designers. Isaia Rankin basically had invented athleisure wear. It was his idea that you should be able to go from a coffee shop to a gym to the club. [He made] one of the first pieces by a Black designer I acquired online. I’ll never forget that the person selling it said, “This belonged to my aunt. Apparently, this guy used to be somebody,” which I just think is the saddest thing. He had this profound impact and then had been largely forgotten.

During the pandemic, when everyone worked from home with too much time on their hands and easy access to shop online, I ended up acquiring quite a bit, in part because a lot of the brick-and-mortar stores, particularly in Europe, were really struggling. They were so reliant on tourist foot traffic, and so a lot of them started selling online [for the first time]. And so there were a number of French boutiques I started acquiring pieces from, including some Patrick Kelly pieces that still have the original store tags from the 1980s, which I won’t remove. Those I won’t wear. The rest of them I wear when they fit. As life goes, they don’t always fit forever.

I’ve become friendly with Patrick Kelly’s widower, Bjorn Amelan, who’s a lovely, wonderful man and the keeper of Patrick’s legacy. It is important to me, when possible, to try to maintain relationships with the people I view as keepers of the legacies because I see my collection as serving the legacy of the designers. The pieces don’t belong to me. They belong to our wider community because they’re a part of our lived history.

The really cool thing is how many people I’ve met along this journey who have not only become cheerleaders but actually reach out to me about pieces. Writer Jill Kargman has become a friend and she gifted me a vintage Stephen Burrows dress. She said, “It belongs in your collection,” which is just the coolest thing ever.

I’m from Houston, and we have the Houston Museum of African American Culture here. We’re living in a really interesting time where people are having conversations about the politics of diversity, but one of the things that’s cool about fashion is that people of different political persuasions were like, “I think this collection’s really cool. I read an article about your collection, I ended up ordering a book about Elizabeth Keckley, or I ended up going down a rabbit hole about Ann Lowe.” At this moment, to be able to do an exhibition like this in my hometown felt particularly poignant.

Melanie Lawson has been a legendary anchor in Houston for decades. She was asked what she’s going to do in retirement, and she said that her husband, John Guess Jr., was the CEO of the Houston Museum of African American Culture. I sent him a note about how much Melanie inspired me and then mentioned my collection.

It was just kismet. They’ve actually never done a fashion exhibition. It was a real gamble. But what I so appreciated is that they had the same vision that, in a time in which our world is so divided, it’s really beautiful when you can find pieces of art that people can come together around.

This interview has been edited and condensed.

#6812. Jamaica Revisited: A New Expression of Caribbean Luxury

Sandals Resorts elevates its robust amenities across the properties of its home island.

There are places in the sun that are just a little more special than the rest. Jamaica is one of them, with powder-sand shores lined with luxury resorts; a dense, verdant interior laced with waterfalls and coffee farms; waters that range from the lightest turquoise to the darkest navy; and a distinctive cuisine layered with spice. The beaches are spectacular and the natural wonders such as the crystalline YS Falls and the winding Martha Brae River are just that—full of awe and adventure.

The Taíno name for Jamaica is Xaymaca, which translates to “the land of wood and water”—and few descriptions capture the island more distinctly. For Sandals Resorts, that ethos has inspired a homegrown luxury. Born in Jamaica more than four decades ago, the brand remains deeply rooted in the island’s familial spirit, personality, food, and topography, even as its properties continue to evolve beyond their birthplace. The result is an immersive travel experience shaped by beaches, lush mountains, local flavors, deep culture, and the people who make Jamaica an unforgettable destination.

From the energy of Montego Bay and Ocho Rios to the sweeping coastline of Negril and the more tranquil pockets of the South Coast, the seven Sandals resorts across Jamaica each offer distinct ways to settle into the island. Across the collection properties, accommodations function as private retreats within a bigger resort experience. Swim-Up Suites allow guests to slide directly from the terrace into the water. Private pools create an aquatic world unto themselves. And on the Caribbean Sea, the Overwater Butler Villas turn a quiet resting place above the water into a pampered vacation lifestyle.

This year, a trio of properties are entering significantly new chapters following gorgeous reimaginings. The iconic comebacks promise renewed accommodations, including new Swim-Up suites, and new dining options. Cocooned by nature thanks to its setting beside a 500-acre preserve, Sandals Jamaica South Coast reopens November 18, with Overwater Butler Villas and the brand’s first Two Queen Junior Suites—perfect for friend-group travel. One month later, December 18 sees the return of Sandals Jamaica Montego Bay, introducing a redesigned pool, Sandals Jamaica Caribbean Cay features newly refreshed accommodations, which are luxurious cocoons. Some days the hardest decision you make might be whether to head for the beach, dine at your favorite restaurant, or remain in your robe exactly where you are.

At Sandals Jamaica Montego Bay, the resort where the brand first began, renewed ocean-view suites promise an impressive vantage point to view the gorgeous ocean. The new Bay Roc Rum Club embraces the music and rum culture that’s part of the fabric of Jamaica and the Caribbean.

Nearby, the former Sandals Jamaica Royal Caribbean emerges as Sandals Jamaica Caribbean Cay with a unique resort experience centered around the sea and water. The new SkyPool Suites will feature a private plunge pool with an infinity edge that’s cantilevered from a large open air terrace, allowing guests to swim while enjoying uninterrupted views of the white sand and aqua waters below.

Further south, the secluded Sandals Jamaica South Coast offers ultra-luxury villas, including detached overwater villas arranged in a heart shape over the calm lagoon. Each comes equipped with see-through glass floor panels, outdoor overwater hammocks, and private boat dock access for the ultimate ocean-to-door service.

Jamaican food rarely arrives without presence. Scotch bonnets bring the heat, pimento adds the spice, and jerk carries the intensity of wood and smoke long before the plate lands on the table. That sense of heritage is part of the experience at Sandals Jamaica Caribbean Cay with Suppa, a new restaurant inspired by the island ritual of Sunday dinner. The menu celebrates familiar Jamaican flavors in savory dishes, stews, and curries served in a casual, supper-style setting made for sharing plates around the table. The adjoining Keynote Rum Bar features a soundtrack of the island’s legends in a laid-back lounge drenched in warm leather and rich tones, plus an extensive Caribbean rum selection.

The waterfront Buccan at Sandals Jamaica Montego Bay brings a different kind of fire. This open air space is where meats, seafood, and veggies are cooked over an open hearth and served at family-style tables set by the ocean. The well-received concept first debuted at the lush and secluded Sandals Saint Vincent, nestled in its own private cove, and is now arriving in Jamaica.

Visiting Sandals Jamaica South Coast, every beach day can start with Jamaica’s renowned Blue Mountain coffee and island pastries at BLŪM before afternoons stretch into hours at The Jerk Shack, enjoying must-have Jamaican jerk chicken, jerk pork, fish in foil, and other heritage-style comfort snacks. At the newly arrived Butch’s Island Chop House, excellent steak dinners and perfectly chilled martinis are on the menu.

Arguably one of the greatest luxuries while on a getaway is not having to weigh every decision. Sandals’ all-inclusive model is built around freedom. Not only are meals, snacks, and drinks included, but so are many of the resort experiences that turn a beach trip into an adventure, such as snorkeling, paddleboarding, kayaking, and daily diving for certified scuba divers, as well as classes to become PADI certified. Back on land, guests can move from the beach to an active day at the tennis and pickleball courts. For the golf lover, a day at Sandals Ochi, Sandals Dunn’s River, and Sandals Royal Plantation in Jamaica also include complimentary greens fees at Upton Estate Golf & Country Club in the green hills above Ocho Rios.

All-inclusive leaves room for every traveler to have their own version of the Caribbean holiday. One guest may spend the morning beneath the surface of the sea and the afternoon learning pickleball, then indulging in fresh sushi and sashimi. Another may decide the day’s only pursuit should be moving from a suite to a lounge chair, then being served jerk chicken or just-out-the-oven pizza beachside.

For all of Jamaica’s waterfalls, rivers, lagoons, beaches, and mountains, the element travelers often cherish most is a feeling of welcome. The ease of a conversation with the butler, the waiter who remembers your daily coffee order, the laugh with the bartender that extends a quick interaction into a memory, the recommendation to try a local dish, visit a secluded beach or a local shop or market that might have otherwise been missed is waiting in Jamaica.

That kind of Jamaican hospitality at Sandals cannot be engineered by simply refreshing a pool or designed into a new suite. It comes from people knowing how to welcome guests to their home. At Sandals Resorts, the flavor, the memorable landscapes, and the contagious Jamaican warmth makes visitors feel less like they simply arrived at a destination—and more like they’ll be welcomed back again and again.Book your Jamaican getaway today at sandals.com.

#6808. We Spent Two Years Testing Mattresses—These Are the Best for Back Pain

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

We found pressure-relieving picks for every sleep style and budget.

A great mattress turns your bedtime routine into the best part of your day. But a subpar mattress will make you dread turning in. We all deserve somewhere to rest that eases our aches and pains. After over two years of testing, the editors of House Beautiful are ready to reveal the best mattresses for back pain on the market.

We spent at least six months sleeping on each mattress below, evaluating their firmness, support, and relief on the pressure points. Every editor has a different sleep need: couples prioritize motion isolation, while stomach sleepers need enough firmness to keep their spine aligned.

Now, you may be thinking that mattresses designed to aid in joint support are going to be extremely expensive, and we won't lie to you; some of the mattresses on our list are pricey (the most costly one is just over $3,000). However, three of our top picks are well under $1,000, so if you want to save while still looking out for your back, you've come to the right place. Otherwise, feel free to splurge because these luxury mattresses are well worth it. And most of our picks come with a lengthy trial period, so you can take your time making sure it’s right for you.

Trust us; if you haven't been sleeping very well for more than a few weeks, your mattress may be to blame. Scroll through our list, read our honest thoughts on these mattresses, and add your favorite one to your cart.

Although Saatva's Rx mattress is "designed for sleepers with chronic back and joint conditions," the comfort level creates a dream sleep experience for all. Just ask tester Katelyn Lunders, who is a firm believer in the mattress after sleeping comfortably night after night for the past two years.

"As a combination sleeper with sciatica, I have found the Rx incredibly supportive and comfortable, whether I sleep on my back or side," she says. "Thanks to the mattress's seven (yes, seven) layers, the firmness isn't one note, like my former memory foam mattress. What sets the Rx apart from other mattresses on the market is its additional layers of pressure-relieving support. The Rx offers more support in the center third of the mattress to help align your spine while you sleep and has a gel-infused memory foam layer to support your lower back. Each section of the mattress works together to relieve pressure points and create a feeling of being cradled, which I love.

I currently sleep alone, so I get out to sprawl out across my Rx, but when I have shared the bed, I have experienced no issues with the edge support. Unlike some mattresses that will roll you off as you attempt to get out of bed in the morning, the Saatva Rx holds steady, thanks to firm spring coils around the mattress's perimeter. The coils provide support without sacrificing comfort due to the layer of foam on top of them.”

When you think of a mattress designed to soothe joint and back pain, you may picture a hefty price tag attached, but this Zinus find is the one exception. At only $230 (for a queen-size), this pick gets the job done for a fraction of the price. Just ask Lunders, who says, “I was personally so happy with my bed-in-a-box memory foam mattress that I told anyone who would listen to buy one, including my own family, who share my love of a good deal.” As for the back pain component, a firm mattress will do wonders, and Lunders notes that this mattress is definitely not soft. “I slept on my Zinus mattress for five years, and it never sagged. If anything, it became more comfortable with time,” she adds.

One more thing about the pressure relief component: Zinus enlisted its patented zoned pressure relief technology designed to help reduce even the smallest aches and pains that may arise throughout the night.

Not everyone has the budget for a luxury mattress that costs thousands of dollars, so if you want an under-$500 mattress that is supportive and holds up well over time, check out contributing writer Kelsey Mulvey's pick.

“As far as I’m concerned, Allswell’s Luxe Hybrid Mattress offers the best bang for your buck,” Mulvey says. “I mean, the King mattress I have costs under $500! It’s sometimes hard to find bedding for that price point! Layers of foam—think a copper-infused memory foam and a shock-absorbing high-density support foam—are paired with an eight-inch coil unit to create the perfect medium-firm base. It’s soft and cloud-like yet provides the right amount of structure, which my husband and I really appreciate.”

“We first got this mattress back in 2019, and it has endured a pandemic, a move, an engagement, and many lazy nights watching movies in bed. While we have experimented with different mattress pads and bed frames, this pick has stayed in tip-top shape and has no obvious sags or dips.”

Tester Samantha Jones was waking up with shoulder and back pain most mornings. But when she tested out the cooling Leesa Sapira Hybrid mattress, that changed.

“Its squishy memory foam layers on top provide a sink-in feeling like you're climbing into a bed at a luxury hotel,” Jones says. “The individually wrapped coils below did their job of providing support for my hips, shoulders, and back, making the bed comfy yet not like quicksand. Notoriously someone who tosses and turns for hours before falling asleep, I've actually found that I drift off to sleep faster on the Sapira Hybrid than my previous, firmer mattress model.”

“I have been waking up with significantly less back pain on the Sapira Hybrid. As an avid runner, I'm accustomed to waking up with tight muscles after a workout. But because the individually wrapped coils contour to my body and provide adequate support for my hips at night, I've noticed less lower back pain and stiffness. The same is true for my shoulder pain: The balance of squish and support has helped eliminate shoulder discomfort.”

Senior Commerce Editor Marina Liao’s customizable Naturepedic mattress allowed her to build a bespoke sleep routine. “I initially slept on the cushion-firm layers and liked them, but after a few weeks, I noticed they didn’t feel as comfy, especially when my sciatica flared up,” Liao says. “The layers suddenly felt too soft for me. Naturepedic offers a 100-night sleep trial, which gives you ample time to test the mattress thoroughly. I switched my layers for the ‘extra firm’ option and immediately liked their feel much better.”

“The mattress feels soft when you’re lying on it, and though you might intuitively think this means you’ll wake up with an achy back, that hasn’t been my experience. The layers ensure you feel supported from the top of your head to your neck, spine, and even hips. I’ll also add that this mattress has a 12-inch height, so it is taller than my old mattress, which makes me feel elevated off the ground and like I’m sleeping on a cloud.”

As a stomach sleeper, Social Media Editor Bianca Bonetti is used to waking up with back pain. She sleeps comfortably since switching to the pillow-top Estate, and to her, it feels like every pressure point is supported.

“Considering I sleep in a position that is bad for my back, it’s kind of a miracle that I haven’t woken up with soreness since sleeping on the Estate mattress,” Bonetti says. "Unless it’s the day after a tough workout. I was hoping to get the best of both worlds by still feeling cocooned and like I’m sleeping on a cloud, but looking out for the health of my back and posture,” Bonetti says. “I love that I get the low back support I need without sinking into the mattress. I do think I would have purchased a medium pillow top if it had been an option for the full size, but I was pleasantly surprised by my love for a firm mattress.”

Shopping Editor Jessica Dukes is pretty picky about her bed and bedding. Comfort and support have to come first, as she has sciatica that doesn’t respond well to poofy mattresses. So the Casper Dream was her ideal switch.

“Night after night, I have found this mattress to be extremely comfortable,” Dukes says. “I also didn't notice too much of a difference between how it felt the first night and how it feels several weeks in, so if you don't want to wait up to 48 hours to start using it, I think that's okay. Despite my back issues, the medium firmness of the memory foam is a delight, which is high praise considering how picky I am. The mattress gives me a slight ‘sinking in’ feeling, but it's supportive at the same time. The result is balanced comfort, and whether I fall asleep on my side or back, I wake up with zero back pain. As for my husband's tossing and turning, I barely feel him move, as those coils do a great job of minimizing the effects.”

If you've ever seen a video of someone dropping an uncooked egg onto a purple grid and being amazed that the egg didn't immediately explode, you're familiar with the mattress brand Purple. That famous purple grid lives beneath the surface of most of its products, as it's the backbone (pun intended) of its creations. The grid in question is what gives you that cradled support ideal for anyone who likes a little extra TLC on their backs. “Given that I weigh much more than an egg, I was skeptical. But after a year of sleeping on the mattress, I'm officially a convert,” says Associate Shopping Editor Janae McKenzie.

“Just above the supportive coils is the purple GelFlex grid, the feature Purple is best known for. The Premier has the thickest grid, so it gives the softest hold,” McKenzie adds. “It's the first mattress I've tested that lives up to its promise to cradle me. Plus, the isolated coils provide pinpoint relief, almost like the mattress can sense where I need extra support and deliver it.” With Purple, you don't really have to settle for a firm mattress (if that's not your thing) to protect your back.

There may not be white-glove delivery involved for the Nectar mattress, which comes in a box and unrolls in 24 hours for you to sleep on, but it feels just as nice as some of the other luxury models on the market. "I grew up sleeping on rock-hard mattresses, so I felt dubious about foam options, thinking they would feel too soft and hurt my back. My Nectar has completely changed my mind about this. Now I can't even go back to sleeping on the surfaces I've previously slept on," Liao says. Nectar offers its mattress in three different tiers with various features: Nectar, Nectar Premier, and Nectar Premier Cooper, which you can read all about on the website. For a brief overview, though, the models differ in height and offer relief for different types of sleepers.

"We opted for just the Nectar in a queen size, which is a 12-inch mattress with cooling capabilities," Liao says. "I would love to take credit for finding the mattress, but it was actually my husband who bought it six years ago while we were still dating for his apartment. I asked him why he bought it, and he said, 'It had good reviews on Reddit or something. I don't remember, but I love it.' I have to concur because it does check the boxes that are important to us, which are comfort and support, as both of us have sciatica.

The mattress is indeed medium-firm, and even after countless nights of rest, it has maintained this feel. When we get up to use the bathroom at night, neither of us feels the other waking up, which speaks to the multiple layers of the Nectar that minimize motion transfer. Both of us have experienced periodic night sweats on this mattress (when our AC breaks or when we're stressed about work), so it's not as cooling as the description says, but with the right sheets (linen!), it does feel cooler. However, if you run extra hot, you may want to upgrade to the Nectar Premier Copper, which has triple-action cooling power.I really love my Nectar, and when it comes time to change it, I'll just buy a Nectar again. In fact, several people who have slept on this bed actually ended up purchasing it right after, including my husband's cousin, who was then pregnant with her third child. She immediately bought it for her and her husband after napping on it!"

How We Selected the Most Comfortable Mattresses

As you might've guessed, the phrase "most comfortable" is subjective when you're talking about a mattress. We all have different sleep preferences, so there isn't a one-size-fits-all approach to shopping for one. In order to know which mattresses are actually comfy and which are not, we started by asking our in-house experts: House Beautiful's very own editors. Many of us test beds for a living (it's part of our jobs), so we only recommend the ones we truly stand by. Of course, in our research, some editors candidly said they don't like their mattresses, so you won't find those named on our best-of list.

After we had our pool of editors who love their mattresses, we made sure everyone had slept on theirs for at least six months. You can only tell if the mattress is truly comfortable over a period of time by sleeping on it every day, which is why plenty of the brands on our list have long sleep trial periods. Additionally, you can rest assured that everyone on the House Beautiful team has different sleeping habits, which translates nicely to a well-rounded curation. You'll find comfortable mattresses for those with back pain, comfortable mattresses for side sleepers, and even comfortable mattresses for the whole family (pets included!). Every editor also provided pros and cons for their mattresses, so you have all the details to make the most informed purchase decision.

What to Look for in the Most Comfortable Mattress

There are several qualities to take note of when you're shopping for a comfortable mattress. They are:

Mattress Type: You will notice that the mattresses on our list are either foam or a hybrid. Foam mattresses are made of memory foam and other layers of foam without any springs or internal structures. Meanwhile, hybrid mattresses typically have coils or an innerspring along with a comfortable layer on top. This layer can be made from various materials, from polyfoam or latex to memory foam. One type isn't better than the other since personal preference will play a role here.

Memory foam is known for conforming to your body's shape and cradling you while providing support. You don't have to worry about squeaky springs (there are none), and the dense foam layers make it hypoallergenic because dust and mites will find it hard to penetrate the deep layers. Some drawbacks of memory foam, though, include trapping heat while you sleep and less edge support. This means you sink in if you sit on the edge of the mattress since memory foam doesn't have coils for support.

On the flip side, since a hybrid has a coil support system, it will feel sturdy and supportive. There's less chance of your mattress sagging over time, and hybrids can support heavier weights. The coils, too, allow for more airflow, so your mattress holds in less heat. Some downsides to hybrids: they are often more expensive than foam mattresses, and they are usually heavier to maneuver.

Firmness: Everyone has a different tolerance for firmness; some prefer a softer feel, while others like a rock-hard mattress. Firmness is calculated by how far one sinks into a mattress. And remember: firmness does not equate to support. The latter is determined by how the mattress helps a sleeper maintain spinal alignment. So, you can have a firm bed but not have it be supportive for your back. To test for support in person, lie on the mattress for at least 10 to 15 minutes—the mattress should push back slightly to support your body. You will know if the mattress supports your body or not after sleeping on it for a few nights. If you get aches and pains anywhere, that means the mattress is not supportive.

Temperature Regulation: If you're a hot sleeper, you know how important it is to find a mattress that is more cooling, which often comes in the form of a hybrid model. (The coils don't trap as much heat as multiple layers of an all-foam mattress.) Look for a mattress with elements like a breathable cover made of cotton or natural latex that regulates body temperature. Mattresses with gel-infused memory foam are also good indicators that they'll cool you as you sleep.

Motion Isolation: If you sleep alone, this probably isn't important to you, but if you sleep with a partner or pet, pay attention to this category. A mattress that can reduce motion transfer will help you get a good night's rest, whether your partner is getting out of bed early for a workout (and you want to sleep in) or your pet is jumping on/off the bed throughout the night. All the picks on our list have excellent motion isolation, but a simple test you can do at home is to put a cup of water on the bed. Press your hands down on one side or even dramatically plop onto the bed—if the glass of water doesn't spill or the liquid barely moves in the cup, the mattress has good motion isolation.

Trial Period: Almost all mattress brands will offer a lengthy trial period for testing out your new piece. You can't sleep on a mattress for a week and expect it to still feel the same months down the line! We love brands that offer a year-long trial, but chances are, when you buy a mattress and it works, you likely won't go through the hassle of returning it (unless it is really terrible!). Make sure to check the return policy and how to return your mattress if needed, as this can affect whether or not you want to buy it.

What to Consider When Buying the Most Comfortable Mattress

Don't forget these personal preferences when shopping for a mattress. They will play a role in what you end up buying.

Budget: When buying any big piece of furniture, price is always a concern. You may not have the budget to spend thousands of dollars on a comfy mattress, but that doesn't mean you should sacrifice your sleep for a more inferior model. Thankfully, our editors all had different budgeting constraints in mind when buying mattresses, so our list contains comfy mattresses that give you the most for your buck to more luxurious options that make you feel like you're staying at a five-star hotel. You can find a quality and supportive mattress for under $500!

Sleeping Position: Now that you're familiar with the different types of mattresses, what is your sleep position? If you're a side sleeper, for instance, memory foam and hybrids are good for pressure-relieving comfort. If you're a heavier sleeper, you may want to opt for a hybrid since the coils will provide more support. If you like your mattress to feel very stiff, then an all-foam option may not be the best for you. Stomach sleepers, like one of our editors, will need medium-firm to firm support, which foam and hybrid will provide. We highlighted the comfiest mattresses for the different types of sleepers above, but again, you won't know until you try one out for yourself. Make sure to read through our notes and the product description, as a good brand will note whether or not this is good for all types of sleeping positions or just caters to one.

Body Type: Similar to how your height affects the type of mattress you buy (you don't want your feet dangling off!), your weight will also factor in the purchase decision. Sleepers who weigh 130 pounds or less can handle a softer mattress because they don't sink too deep into the mattress. If you weigh between 130 and 230 pounds, go for a medium-soft to medium-firm mattress. For those who weigh more than 230 pounds, a firmer mattress with extra support is ideal.

How to Pick the Most Comfortable Mattresses for Pain

Chances are, if you're looking for a comfortable mattress, it's because you've been sleeping on a mattress that gives you back pain, or you have a pre-existing condition that makes picking a mattress trickier. Here, we break down what to look for if you have:

Back pain: Look for a mattress with a medium-firm to firm surface. Studies show that this might effectively reduce back pain in individuals. Mattresses like the ones from Saatva even spotlight that they're good for people with back pain and are chiropractor-approved. Look for these types of callouts in your mattress as you shop.

Hip pain: The best mattresses for hip pain offer firm lumbar support with gentle pressure relief. The mattresses should have a supportive edge and be high enough to allow your hips to remain above your knees when you're sitting on them. Memory foam is often good for hip pain as it contours to your body, but keep in mind that if you're a stomach or back sleeper with hip pain, this material might allow too much sinkage, and your spine won't be properly aligned—going with a hybrid model then is more beneficial.

Shoulder pain: For people with shoulder pain, we didn't forget about you! Look for memory foam or latex mattresses that will provide pressure relief and support as they contour to your body the best. In particular, if you're a side sleeper with shoulder pain, opt for a softer mattress vs. a firmer one. Also, consider switching out your pillow, as the wrong one will misalign your spine, causing both neck and shoulder pain. Shop our best pillows for neck pains.

House Beautiful editors are experts in all things bedding, from having written about the softest sheets to reviewing the most luxurious mattresses. Our editors have tested and thoroughly reviewed popular mattresses from the likes of Nolah, Saatva, Purple, and more, plus traded notes with one another so we can recommend the best options to readers. For this story on comfortable mattresses, since the topic is entirely dependent on personal preferences, we asked our fellow editors for their top-rated picks that they themselves own and have slept on for months to years at a time. There is no better indicator that a mattress is comfortable if you've had it for a long time, and it helps alleviate any pain! We spent over a week making sure the list was packed with a variety of brands and mattress types so readers could find the one that most closely matches their own personal preferences. Every mattress on this list is thoroughly vetted by the team.

#6807. Andrew Garfield and Monica Barbaro’s Complete Relationship Timeline

Awards season brought love into Andrew Garfield’s life. On Feb. 21, 2025, People reported that Garfield has been “quietly” dating A Complete Unknown’s Monica Barbaro after the two were seen together in London.

A source told People that “they’ve been really low-key and have been spending time together quietly.” The actors’ reps did not respond to People’s request for comment.

In October 2024, Garfield told Esquire that he protects his love life, so don’t expect him to comment on any dating rumors. “I have never, and I won’t ever, speak about or confirm or deny anything about my personal life with anyone, ever,” he said.

Garfield, 43, was last romantically linked to spiritual adviser Dr. Kate Tomas; she confirmed their breakup on Oct. 11, 2024.

Barbaro, meanwhile, is a 36-year-old rising star who hasn’t spoken much about her love life either. She has touched on some offscreen relationships though, telling L’Officiel Australia in July 2023 that she has “a lovely community of friends. I love to host at my place and have everyone over for a Sunday morning, wear your PJs and just come, hang out and watch whatever, sports game or chat, catch up....Luckily, after abandoning my friends so much and leaving town, they’re still willing to come over and hang out.”

And in February 2025, she told Hollywood Authentic that attending events during awards season can really “change an actor’s life.”

When she went to the 2023 Oscars with the Top Gun: Maverick cast, “I just kept thinking: ‘Embrace it. See it. Feel it,’” she said.

“One of the coolest things about being in that conversation is getting to have that sense of community in a space that can be quite intimidating professionally,” she continued. “It was just so exciting to be there, and to watch people make speeches and honor their fellow nominees, and really truthfully do so. It’s not fake. That was so cool.”

Here, the complete (public) history of Barbaro and Garfield’s pretty private romance so far.

A source said the two actors had been dating quietly but did not specify when their relationship began. “They’ve been really low-key and have been spending time together quietly,” the insider said.

Neither commented on dating reports when they came out. Garfield does not have social media, while Barbaro has remained silent on the topic on Instagram.

The couple was not photographed interacting at the ceremony, nor did they pose together at the Vanity Fair Oscar Party that they both attended. An eyewitness told Entertainment Tonight that they hung out inside there, though.

On November 2, Garfield and Barbaro took in the Los Angeles Lakers’ game against the Miami Heat at Crypto.com Arena. They were photographed sitting courtside together, with Garfield wearing a burgundy top and khakis, while Barbaro chose a white button-down and jeans.

#6805. Amanda Seyfried Finally Responded to All That Oscar Buzz for ‘You Can See Everything’

Never been more invested in awards season.

Did you come away from You Can See Everything ready to invest all your time and resources into Amanda Seyfried’s Oscar campaign? Very much same.

Despite the movie being a documentary, fans are fully on board with Amanda getting a Best Supporting Actress nomination for playing Elizabeth Holmes. Yeah, she was technically being herself playing someone else—but she was still acting and her performance was brilliant.

So, how does Amanda feel about the buzz? She finally weighed in…via Instagram comments.

Nobody Wants This star Timothy Simons recently said what the rest of us are thinking on The Kyle Buchanan Show, musing “I would understand if somebody was like, ‘Well, this is a documentary. It’s not eligible.’ I don’t know how the rules of this work. But one thing that I can say is that both times that I saw it, when she finally walks into the recreation of Nathan visiting her in jail, she does that sort of tippy toe hug and there’s a shot over his shoulder onto her face. Both times that I saw it, my mind had completely melded the two people together in that when I was looking at her, I was looking at Elizabeth Holmes in a way that I can’t describe.” He added, “I don’t know how the rules break out but just what I saw, the performance was incredible.”

Cut to Amanda, who hopped onto Instagram after seeing the conversation and wrote “TIMOTHY” alongside a couple heart-eyes emojis.

As for whether Amanda can technically be nominated for Best Supporting Actress in a documentary, Variety reports that (1) she certainly can, and (2) A24 is actively launching a campaign for her. Love to see it.

#6801. Inside the Lavish Estates Where The Gilded Age Was Filmed

These locations are straight out of 1880s high society.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Why should the Brits have all the fun? The writer of Downton Abbey, Julian Fellowes, went stateside with the HBO series The Gilded Age, which tells the story of New York City high society in the 1880s amid the halcyon days of the industrial boom. The series explores the tension between old-money elites and their escalating feud with the nouveau riche neighbors who threaten the established social order. Meanwhile in real life, powerhouse families like the Vanderbilts and the Carnegies left their opulent footprints on Newport, Rhode Island, in the form of their famously ostentatious and absolutely gargantuan “summer cottages.” Circumstances which lend themselves to set-design magic.

In honor of season four of The Gilded Age, which premiered November 1, we’re revisiting the real-life settings that have helped bring the series to life. The social alliances may shift, but the architecture remains a dependable scene-stealer. Below, tour 21 properties that rank among the East Coast’s architectural crown jewels, from historic house museums to lavish mansions.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s ballroom and George Russell’s bedroom, as well as the billiard room.

These so-called dollar princesses and their families lived in Manhattan and spent their summers in Newport, Rhode Island. Of course, these industry magnates didn’t consider their infrequent visits (some only came a few weeks in a year) as a reason to exercise restraint. The mammoth Breakers mansion is one of four so-called summer “cottages” (read: castles) that were completed just blocks from each other in a building boom between 1892 and 1902. The mansion was designed by Richard Morris Hunt in the Renaissance Revival style, as a less-than-subtle symbol of the Vanderbilt family’s social and financial preeminence in the Gilded Age. It was architectural one-upmanship that is considered the grandest of all the manses, featuring ornate ballrooms, extensive art collections, manicured gardens, and the latest (then) in modern technology, including electric lights. It has 70 rooms, an interior using marble imported from Italy and Africa, and a library mantel purchased from a château in France.

“When you walk into the dining room of The Breakers, with its painting of the goddess Aurora on the ceiling 50 feet above you or see 500,000 cubic feet of marble in a variety of different hues, you can’t help but be awestruck by the incredible wealth of these Gilded Age tycoons,” says Trudy Coxe, the chief executive officer and executive director of The Preservation Society of Newport County, which has under its stewardship six of the grand mansions featured in the series.

The preservation society worked closely with the filming crew to ensure the safety of the objects and spaces. “We had long discussions about the billiard table at The Breakers,” she explains. “It could not be removed, but it was important to the scene being filmed. Rather than moving it, we worked with their props department to put an alternative covering on the table to simulate an actual game of pool.”

In the series, the Breakers’ neo–Italian Renaissance great hall and music room are featured as Bertha Russell’s (played by Carrie Coon) ballroom, which we see in the final ball scene. George Russell (Morgan Spector) also discusses business while playing billiards in the mansion’s massive billiard room.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s bedroom in the Russell residence

Marble House was built from 1888 to 1892 as a summer cottage for Alva and William Kissam Vanderbilt. It was also designed by Richard Morris Hunt, but in the Beaux-Arts style. For an American house, it was unparalleled in opulence, with a temple-front portico resembling that of the White House, which had been completed 92 years earlier in 1800 (lofty, no?).

Mrs. Russell’s bedroom, built on a soundstage in Bethpage, New York, was inspired by real-life socialite Alva Vanderbilt’s bedroom in the Marble House. The French Louis XV bed was painted and reupholstered in salmon-pink velvet, and the custom bedding used 300 yards of fabric that have been draped, cinched, and swagged.

And that’s just the textiles. Most of the room’s furniture is refurbished antique and everything in the room has been painted to match. Sometimes, Shaw notes, it was difficult to paint a lovely wooden antique piece. “The scenic artists would look at us and say, ‘You really want me to paint over the chestnut? Are you sure?’” says Shaw. “Yes.”

Consuelo Vanderbilt’s (Alva and William’s child) monochrome red bedroom in the mansion also serves as George Russell’s bedroom in the show.

Features as: The exterior of Sylvia Chamberlain’s house

Rosecliff was built for Nevada silver heiress Theresa Fair Oelrichs, whose father, James Graham Fair, was one of the four partners in the Comstock Lode. The famed architectural firm McKim, Mead & White was commissioned to design the mansion, which was modeled after the Grand Trianon, the garden retreat of French kings at Versailles. The French Baroque Revival gem was designed for entertaining at a grand scale—and it certainly fulfilled its purpose. Fair Oelrichs would become known as one of the three great hostesses of Newport.

The white front facade of this mansion, which recently got a $7.4 million renovation, is a stand-in for Sylvia Chamberlain’s (played by Jeanne Tripplehorn) house. You’ll see it in Episode 7, when Tom Raikes (Thomas Cocquerel) pays a visit.

Features as: The servant kitchen and the real-life Sarah Berwind’s bedroom as parts of the Russell residence

The Elms is a Classical Revival dwelling that was completed in 1901. Architect Horace Trumbauer designed it for the coal baron Edward Julius Berwind, taking inspiration from the 18th-century d’Asnières French château. It also houses an impressive collection of paintings and tapestries, as well as the latest technology of its age.

In The Gilded Age Seasons 1 and 2, The Elms stands in as part of the Russells’ lavish residence, including scenes in the kitchen and Bertha Russell’s bedroom. But it truly steps into the spotlight in Season 3, when it hosts a dazzling ball. And as a long-awaited confession of love looms (we won’t spoil from whom), the opulent Newport mansion proves to be the perfect setting for high drama and high society.

Each guest is granted a cinematic entrance, illuminated by a golden glow that bathes the grand façade—a new addition this season, as electricity is now becoming available. “The house’s interiors would not have been electrified yet, but people were beginning to use it for parties,” Shaw says. “That would mean they would have to have their own generator installed somewhere on the property to light up those lights.” To accomplish this, the crew went to specialty shops that make reproduction bulbs. “The original bulbs were much bigger than what we have in a household today,” Shaw adds. “Some of the lightbulbs we used were 10 inches in diameter, which is how big they were then.”

Features as: The exterior is used for Caroline Astor’s Beechwood house; the boardinghouse is used for a scene with Oscar van Rhijn; and another room serves as Agnes van Rhijn’s bedroom

Completed in 1852, Château-sur-Mer is one of the first grand Bellevue Avenue mansions of the Gilded Age in Newport, built by architect Seth C. Bradford for William Shepard Wetmore. Its interiors were designed by Ogden Codman Jr., who co-authored Edith Wharton’s Decoration of Houses. The mansion was host to many major parties, including the Fête Champêtre, an elaborate country picnic for more than 2,000 guests in 1857, as well as a debutante ball for Edith Wetmore in 1889. Wetmore’s son, George Peabody Wetmore, went on to have a distinguished political career as the governor of Rhode Island and, later, as a U.S. senator.

In The Gilded Age, the home’s exterior was used for Caroline Astor’s (Donna Murphy) Beechwood House. The hallway, dining room, and ballroom were used during Mamie Fish’s (Ashlie Atkinson) doll tea party. Several bedrooms serve as Agnes van Rhijn’s (Christine Baranski) bedroom, Oscar van Rhijn’s (Blake Ritson) bedroom, Anne Morris’s (Katie Finneran) bedroom, and Patrick Morris’s (Michel Gill) office. There’s also a scene filmed at the boardinghouse with Oscar van Rhijn, according to Patch.

Features as: The office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes

Hunter House was constructed in 1748 at the harborfront near the north end of the Newport Historic District at a time when the city was a bustling seaport in the British empire. The house was owned by wealthy merchants and overlooked the wharves that serviced their trading ships. It’s one of the finest examples of Colonial architecture in Newport, according to the Preservation Society. Inside, its resident families furnished the house with decorative arts produced by expert craftsmen, much of which has been preserved today. Such a luxurious lifestyle, however, depended upon the labor of enslaved people of African descent; recent research by the Preservation Society has identified at least 13 enslaved individuals who lived and worked at Hunter House. Today’s Hunter House honors their perseverance and incalculable contributions to Newport’s past.

One of the sitting rooms in Hunter House functions as the office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes, and is set fictitiously in Doylestown, Pennsylvania.

Features as: The Astor family summer home

Belcourt was completed in 1894 for Oliver Hazard Perry Belmont, a prominent American socialite and coachman who served in the U.S. House of Representatives. Like many of the Newport mansions, the Château-esque structure was completed by architect Richard Morris Hunt; it was designed to mimic the hunting lodge of Versailles, built for Louis XIII of France, but with a special ask to accommodate Oliver’s collection of horses and automobiles. In 1896, Oliver gave the house to his second wife, the former Alva Vanderbilt, who designed the neighboring Newport mansion Marble House. Alva used Belcourt as a gathering place for the elite of society, hosting lavish parties and balls and attracting notable figures in politics, business, and the arts.

In The Gilded Age Season 1, Belcourt shows up in Episode 8 as Mrs. Astor’s lavish new Newport home, which Mrs. Russell sneaks inside and is forced to flee to avoid a less-than-pleasant meetup.

Features as: The social gathering scene where Caroline Astor and George Russell meet

Built in 1867 by prolific Boston tea and china merchant Robert M. Cushing, the Ledges was originally the summer home of the Cushing family. This majestic oceanfront pile is perched on one of Newport’s most desirable peninsulas, overlooking Bailey’s Beach. It was built by architect John H. Sturgis, who designed a large, Stick-style cottage that was equal parts comfort and elegance. Today it features original furnishings, papered walls, and unique artwork, including a studio that was added to the side of the house for Robert’s artistic children.

Today, the estate remains in the hands of the Cushing descendants, who occasionally open the house up to film crews. It was featured a few years ago in the film Evening, starring Glenn Close and Meryl Streep. “Working at the Ledges was pretty extraordinary,” Gilded Age location manager Lauri Pitkus told The Providence Journal, “because I don't think I’ve ever been in a home with spectacular views out of every window.”

This historic house was built in 1864 for John Noble Alsop Griswold, an Old China Trade merchant and diplomat. The house is the work of Richard Morris Hunt; it was one of the earliest buildings in the American Stick style of architecture. The house has a complex roofline with a main mansarded section that is pierced by numerous gable and dormer sections. It boasts a number of balconies sheltered by deep eaves, with gable ends decorated with woodwork. Today, the house is a gallery location for the Newport Art Museum. In the series, the house features in Season 1 Episode 1, and again in Episode 5.

Features as: Mingling with the other elite scene

The International Tennis Hall of Fame and its perfectly preserved grass courts are in the Newport Casino, a National Historic Landmark property. The historic seven-acre estate was commissioned by New York Herald publisher James Gordon Bennett as a social club for the elite summer residents of Newport. The Newport News boasted three days after it opened in July 1880: “It is doubtful if a livelier place can be found.” Today, the space is recognized as the official hall of fame for the sport of tennis globally. Since 1955, 262 distinguished inductees representing 27 countries have been enshrined in Newport.

In the eighth episode of The Gilded Age Season 1, the characters are seen promenading around the piazza that overlooks what is known today as Horseshoe Court. A few characters are even playing tennis. “It was fitting to have that history depicted here, given The Gilded Age’s look into that time period,” the hall of fame’s Megan Erbes tells ELLE DECOR. “Several of our staff members got to work closely with the production team and actors, teaching everything from the history of our property to how to hit a proper forehand.” RI Monthly reported that crews returned to the courts last summer to film Season 2 of the show, so it’s possible that Newport Casino will make another appearance.

Features as: The parlor, library, entryway, and staircase of the Morris house, located on Fifth Avenue in the show; Mr. and Mrs. Morris’s bedrooms were filmed in the Newport mansions

William Smith Slater hired General William Walker to build a home as a wedding gift for his daughter. The result was this historic home, which was completed in 1877. The Victorian house is filled with treasures—the fireplace surrounds, for example, were carved by Charles Dowler, who came to the United States to produce arms for the Civil War. Today, the house is open to visitors and is known as the Clouds Hill Museum.

The Clouds Hill Museum is the only Rhode Island site outside of Newport that was used in the series. The crew scheduled filming for Season 1 in late February of 2021, when a few hundred people were on site during two days of filming, museum director Wayne Cabral told Warwick Online, noting that the taping finished around midnight on the second day of filming (after five days of work to prep the set). Almost all the parlor’s furniture was removed, and the head set designer brought in items sourced from local antiques stores in East Greenwich and North Kingstown, museum president Anne Holst added. The producers were adamant about removing the existing curtains, which were disintegrating, Holst added, so she negotiated to leave the replacements (which cost $10,000) with the museum after filming.

Features as: Visit to the home of Aurora Fane scene

The Gothic Revival–style Lyndhurst Mansion was designed in 1838 by Alexander Jackson Davis. Lyndhurst’s third owner, Jay Gould, is the real-life inspiration for the “new money” character George Russell. “He was fantastically ruthless in business, to an extent that Mrs. Astor would never let him in, he was never allowed into society, and she didn’t let his children in until he was dead,” creator, writer, and producer Julian Fellowes said in a podcast. “But he was an incredibly affectionate father and a very caring and faithful husband…and his children all had these nostalgic memories of Lyndhurst, their house on the Hudson.”

In Season 1 of The Gilded Age, Lyndhurst’s interiors are the home of characters Charles and Aurora Fane (Ward Horton and Kelli O’Hara), who are related to the van Rhijns and part of the “old money” side of society. The Lyndhurst lawns and grounds also doubled as Central Park and the Sheep’s Meadow in the opening sequence. The home boasts a bowling alley that dates back to 1894, which was re-created to be the ferry terminal that Marian Brook (Louisa Jacobson) and Peggy Scott (Denée Benton) take to Manhattan for the first time in Episode 1. And finally, the house’s carriage house was transformed into The New York Globe newspaper offices.

The Belvedere Estate, located between Lyndhurst Mansion and writer Washington Irving’s Sunnyside home, is a century-old mansion on 25 acres in historic Tarrytown. The estate was originally part of Philipsburg Manor, a 90,000-acre property built for Florence and Casper Whitney. “While the mansion dates from the 1920s, it has a much older European feel that could represent something from decades earlier,” says Belvedere Estate sales director Linda Fiorentino, who recalls the massive legwork required to film at the mansion.

“I will never forget the background actors descending from a yellow school bus, fully dressed in period costumes and felt hats,” she says. The Season 1 scene was filmed at the pond at the back of Belvedere Estate, acting as a stand-in for Central Park in the scene when Marian meets Mr. Raikes at Bethesda Fountain in Episode 1. The pond is a man-made feature, and the water level sometimes sinks to a mere foot or two, Fiorentino explains. “Before filming, they filled the pond with roughly 600,000 gallons of water to fill to capacity, giving the illusion of a much larger body of water and to allow them to have pleasure boats filled with passengers on the water,” she says. They also re-created Central Park’s Shakespeare Bridge from rough-hewn logs and brought in trees, shrubs, and foliage to cover the existing link fence because it was too modern.

Features as: The drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain

Toward the end of the 19th century, Colonel Elliott Fitch Shepard, a lawyer who founded the New York State Bar Association in 1876, purchased more than 500 acres on a hill high above the Hudson River north of Tarrytown. He engaged the renowned architecture firm McKim, Mead & White to design his family’s manor house here. The result was a 75-room Victorian structure that mimicked 18th-century Italian villa architecture. Think: hand-carved mahogany paneling, a grand stairway near the front entrance, high patterned ceilings, and elaborate cornice work. Unfortunately, Shepard died before it was completed in 1893, and it passed into the hands of William Rockefeller and Frank Vanderlip. These men were part of a group (including John Jacob Astor, who died on the Titanic in 1912) who went on to convert the building to the Sleepy Hollow Country Club, which remains a private members-only club today.

Scenes in the drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain were shot on location here. “The entire room was redressed by our team in the Rococo style,” set decorator Regina Graves told 1st Dibs.

Features as: Part of Mrs. Astor’s residence

Glenview was built in 1876 by John Bond Trevor, a highly successful stockbroker, who engaged New York City architect Charles W. Clinton to build his estate. The mansion was a paragon of luxury and modern comfort, featuring indoor plumbing, gas illumination, and a coal-burning furnace to circulate heat throughout its 37 rooms. The exterior had elements of Victorian Gothic and French Second Empire design, complete with a mansard roof, a gray stone facade constructed of blocks hewn from a Hastings-on-Hudson quarry, and decorative sandstone elements brought from Indiana.

The mansion makes an appearance as Caroline Astor’s residence in Season 1. “They were all so complimentary about Glenview, and of course we did a lot of polishing and waxing,” Laura Vookles, chair of the museum’s curatorial department, told The Rivertown Enterprise. “They focused on our exquisite interiors and filmed in six rooms, including the dining, library, and sitting rooms, and used all but one painting that is normally on view.”

The John Paine Mansion, also known as the Castle, was built in 1896 and was deemed the grandest house in Troy, New York. It has since served as a private residence, a college administration building, a movie set (see: Martin Scorsese’s 1993 drama The Age of Innocence), and, currently, a fraternity house. In the show, one of the bedrooms in the Castle features as Tom Raikes’s office.

This residence, the home of Troy locals Bill and Sue Comiskey, stands in for the Brooklyn residence of the well-off Scott family. At one point early in the series, Arthur Scott (John Douglas Thompson) and his daughter, Peggy (Denée Benton), sit on carved-oak chairs around a damask-covered dining table. The home’s elaborate details really shine in this historic home, which dates back to the turn of the 19th century. Stained-glass windows and an elegant china cabinet are memorable architectural features that are original to the location and transport the viewers back in time.

While filming what would be the Scott family residence, Bill, the homeowner, told News10 that a few prized family possessions actually made the cut. “My desk was in it, front and center…you [also] saw the whole front and back parlor into the dining room where they were having a meal,” he explained. “[There were] great shots of this room catching the stained glass, the fireplace, [and] this bookcase my grandfather made.”

Features as: The interior is used as George Russell’s workspace

Hempstead House was built for Howard Gould by architects Hunt & Hunt in 1912 on the north shore of Long Island. The mansion was eventually sold to art mogul Daniel Guggenheim (yes, that Guggenheim). Hempstead House is one of four mansions on the grounds of the Guggenheim Estate—which includes Castle Gould, Falaise, and Mille Fleur. The Tudor-style castle was the embodiment of Gold Coast opulence, a place where the esteemed family hosted grand parties, performances, and exhibitions by gatherings of the elite of the time. To say it is massive would be an understatement: 50,000 square feet, with 40 rooms and a 60-foot-tall entry foyer. In The Gilded Age, the mansion’s interior was used as George Russell’s workspace.

Features as: The late General Henry Brook’s house

Rock Hall Museum was initially an 18th-century plantation. The two-and-a-half-story Georgian-style home was built in approximately 1767 and later restored to its original appearance in the 1950s. In Season 1 Episode 1, the house’s white facade is featured within the first 15 minutes as a backdrop for the late General Henry Brook’s house. Marian, his daughter, is seen walking out of the home on her way to the train station.

The Hart Cluett Museum is a work of Federal architecture designed by Martin E. Thompson in 1827. He was hired by William Howard. Thompson brought the design of the Hart Cluett House back to New York City, where many examples of the 1827 Federal-style townhouse were copied throughout Manhattan. This house, a museum open to the public today, was featured in The Gilded Age in Episode 2 as Anne Morris’s home.

Rachel Silva is a freelance writer and editor covering design, architecture, and culture. She previously served as an editor at ELLE Decor, and her work has appeared in Harper’s Bazaar, TIME, and Veranda, among others. A graduate of Columbia Journalism School, she is based in New York City.
