#6765. Who Is Elizabeth Holmes’s Partner, Billy Evans?

This story contains spoilers for You Can See Everything.

When Nathan Fielder asked Elizabeth Holmes to document the days leading up to her serving her prison sentence for his new A24 documentary, You Can See Everything, the proposition meant that he would also be spending a large portion of the shoot with Holmes’s partner, Billy Evans.

The documentary spends time delving into Holmes’s public legal scandal with Theranos—the now-defunct biotech company that saw her committing investment fraud—but it also sheds light on Holmes and Evans’s relationship and how they have managed to thrive despite her sentencing.

As the documentary, co-directed by Lance Oppenheim, progresses, Fielder’s focus shifts from Holmes to Evans. “Weren’t you worried she was scamming you?” Fielder asked him at one point, to which Evans responded, “100 percent, but what does she have to gain from me?” When presented with the knowledge of his family’s wealth, Evans defended Holmes and said, “I think there’s a lot of different levels of wealth.” Things only snowball from there.

Evans’s grandparents, William and Anne Evans, own the Evans Hotel Group. Founded in 1953, Evans Hotels consists of three luxury properties, which include The Lodge Terry Pines, the Bahia Resort Hotel, and the Catamaran Resort Hotel and Spa. All three of the properties are located in California.

Evans’s LinkedIn profile revealed that he attended the Massachusetts Institute of Technology between 2011 and 2015. He earned his bachelor’s degree in economics.

Evans’s LinkedIn also revealed that he’s the CEO of Haemanthus, his self-founded AI-driven medical testing startup; he’s held the role since 2022. The company aims to achieve a mission similar to that of Holmes’s Theranos, which sought to provide blood diagnostics through one drop of blood with the Edison machine. Instead of using one drop of blood for diagnostics, Haemanthus is working to create a device that tests blood in addition to urine and saliva for the presence of diseases.

“This is not Theranos 2.0. Theranos attempted to miniaturize existing tests. Our approach is fundamentally different. We use light to read the complete molecular story in biological fluids, seeing patterns current tests can’t detect. Not an improvement. A different paradigm,” the company shared in a statement to Fortune in 2025, following reports of Holmes advising on the project. “Setting the record straight. Elizabeth Holmes has zero involvement in Haemanthus. We’ve learned from her company’s mistakes, but she has no role, now or future.”

In a 2023 joint interview with The New York Times, Evans revealed that he and Holmes fell for each other fast. “To say we immediately fell in love isn’t an overstatement,” he said.

Holmes, however, noted that Evans had some reservations about entering a relationship with her. “It had been two years of all this stuff written about me, and I think you get to know someone in a totally different way when you walk in with that skepticism versus if you meet when everything is sunshine and roses,” she said. “That allowed us to get to know each other in a really deep way.” The couple got engaged in August 2018.

Holmes gave birth to their son, William Holmes Evans, in 2021. By 2023, a court document revealed that Holmes gave birth to her and Evans’s second child, a daughter named Invicta.

Evans attended several of Holmes’s court dates, appearing by her side on the way to and from her hearings. In January 2022, she was found guilty of three counts of wire fraud and one count of conspiracy to commit wire fraud. She’s currently serving her sentence—which was reduced to nine years from 11 years—at Federal Prison Camp Bryan in Texas. She will be transferred to a halfway house in August 2027.

While Holmes was sentenced, Evans asked the court in November 2022 to shorten her sentence so she could enjoy more special moments with their son. “My heart is broken with the thought of spending any days away from Liz, for a future in which my son grows up with a relationship with his mother on the other side of glass armed by guards,” he said. Evans still visits Holmes regularly, with him telling People in February 2025 that it has been “hell and torture” at the prison facility. The tech CEO added that it “shatters” Holmes when she says goodbye to him and her family.

You Can See Everything makes some shocking revelations about Evans, like that he posts from Holmes’s X account while she’s in prison (for a while, it was without her knowledge), and that he arranged to be paid for his and Holmes’s appearance in the documentary, which he ultimately rescinded. (It’s unclear whether Holmes knew about the deal.)

During a screening of the film in L.A. this month, Fielder said of the couple, “Neither of them have seen the film. Elizabeth is still incarcerated; Billy hasn’t seen it.” He added, according to The Hollywood Reporter, that Evans “started trying to exert a level of control over the process that made it very hard to engage with him, and it was uncomfortable for us and also A24 and everyone. And I think we sort of had to stop—I think communication broke down basically, and we’ve been in that like state for a little bit. So it’s been challenging in that way.”

Evans confirmed that he hasn’t seen the film during an interview with the All In podcast, which was released on October 15. “I haven’t seen it. I might think this is the dumbest thing I have ever done,” Evans said, per Variety. He has seen the trailer and said that Holmes “looks insane” in it. Evans added, “Am I nervous? I have been through so many intense, surreal experiences that it takes a bit to raise my blood pressure. With this, there’s certainly concern, but it’s ultimately our decision.”

On November 3, Evans revealed the “one misconception” about his relationship with Holmes following the release of the movie: “That I somehow control Liz. Anyone who actually knows her would find that idea hilarious,” he responded to an X user.

Evans added, “She’s fiercely independent, strong willed, and my best friend. We are partners in every sense. We challenge each other, disagree, and face the world together. Her closest friends are also some of my closest friends. If I ever mistreated her, they’d have my head.”

In a separate tweet, Evans revealed that he misses “everything about [Holmes].” He wrote, “I miss her presence. Her touch. The way she runs her fingers through my hair. Her patience. She makes me a better person. And without her, I feel an emptiness that’s hard to describe. People often say they need someone when what they really mean is that they want them. I’ve thought a lot about that distinction. I need Liz. She should qualify as a medically necessary prescription.”

On All In, Evans elaborated on why he wanted to make the film ahead of Holmes’s 11-year sentence. “She’s been prevented from speaking publicly in any way, shape, or form, and this would come out after that period in which she is now allowed to talk,” he said. “There’s been a lot of media produced on her. There’s been a lot of people who have told her story. That narrative is pretty ossified. The whole idea was: Here she is, see her and make up your own mind. It’s not to re-litigate the case. Practically, at this time she was not allowed to talk about the case, so she was gonna follow the rules and not talk about the case.”

He explained that his own loved ones have thought he was “crazy” for knowing—and now being in a relationship with—Holmes. “And they would meet her and spend time with her and see her in a totally natural environment, like going on a hike or helping a friend. Every opinion of her would radically shift,” Evans said. “That was the intention of the film. To help scale that up and allow other people to sit with her and make up their own minds.”

#6762. Are ‘Love Is Blind’ Weddings Legally Binding? Everything We Know About the Wedding and Divorce Rules

I need to know just how “married” these couples really are....

Welp, another batch of strangers just had elaborate wedding ceremonies on reality TV! Love Is Blind Season 11 dropped its finale, and we found out which couples got married. This year, John and Sarah ended up saying “I do” at the altar, but were these weddings actually legally binding, or did Netflix pull a Let’s Marry Harry and leave the paperwork out of it? About that...

They most certainly are. The couples are married by real officiants, and real paperwork is filed. Meaning, if things don’t work out they have to go through divorce proceedings just like any other couple. As creator Chris Coelen told Bustle in 2020, "If they choose to get married, yes, it's a legally binding marriage."

Former cast member Lauren Speed also told Refinery29 that she and Cameron Hamilton “definitely did” get married, and that was the “part that was so scary for me. This is a real legally binding marriage. This is not for TV. This is our life."

In fact, the legitimacy of the Love Is Blind weddings is one of the ways the show gets spoiled. TMZ dug into marriage records and spoiled several weddings back in 2020, and fans on Reddit have also been known to sleuth.

According to The Tab, the same day as the wedding. The outlet reports that “it’s all done on the same day, often with a judge or ordained officiant present.”

Couples reportedly have to wait until 11 months after their wedding so that the show isn’t spoiled for viewers. As for who foots the bill, The Tab reports that production pays for up to $5,000 per couple for filings and mediation.

#6761. The Beast in Me Season 2: Everything We Know So Far

Netflix’s The Beast in Me brought Claire Danes back to TV and reunited her with creator Howard Gordon, with whom she spent eight seasons working on Homeland. That combination was already exciting enough, but the cast also included Matthew Rhys and Brittany Snow, and Conan O’Brien and Jodie Foster were among the producers.

Danes stars as Aggie, a successful author grappling with the grief of losing her son. Her fragile sense of stability is shaken when Nile (Rhys), a wealthy real estate figure once accused of murdering his wife, moves in next door. With just eight episodes, the series left viewers wondering whether Aggie’s story could continue beyond its initial run. Here’s what we know so far.

Yes. In September 2026, Variety confirmed that The Beast in Me would return for a second season on Netflix.

“We always told ourselves we’d only do a second season of The Beast in Me if we found a story that was worthy of the first—and I’m thrilled to say we found it,” Gordon said in a statement. “We loved making this show and our audience seemed to love watching it, so we’re enormously grateful to Jinny Howe and Netflix, and to Karey Burke and 20th Television for giving us the chance to get the band back together and do it again.”

Variety’s report confirmed that Danes would return to her role for The Beast in Me season 2. In October 2026, Deadline reported that John Goodman (Righteous Gemstones, Digger) would star as “Aggie’s dad, believed to be a long-time con artist.”

In November 2026, Deadline revealed that Lili Taylor (Outer Range, The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping) joined the cast in a series regular role as Fiona, Aggie’s stepmother.

Gordon discussed the ending of season 1 with The Wrap and what it made him consider for season 2.

“I think that to the extent that Aggie is a writer who has been delivered to this next stage of her life, whatever that stage is,” he shared at the time. “If there is a story, there might be more. I am tempted by the character of her grifter father in some way. We’ll see how the show does and if an idea comes. But I think they’d be open to more.”

#6760. Where Is Joran van der Sloot Now? The Truth Behind Netflix’s ‘Taken: Natalee Holloway’

The new documentary revisits the 2005 disappearance that gripped the world.

Taken: Natalee Holloway is a new three-part Netflix documentary about the disappearance of 18-year-old Natalee Holloway, who vanished during a trip to Aruba in 2005. The series revisits the case through the eyes of her mother, Beth, who has never spoken in such detail about her ordeal before, along with the people who spent years searching for answers.

One question has followed the case for more than two decades: What really happened to Natalee, and what became of Joran van der Sloot, the man who admitted killing her but was never actually punished for it?

Joran van der Sloot is a Dutch man who was 17 when Natalee Holloway disappeared during what should have been a celebratory holiday with friends; he was one of the last people known to have seen her.

Natalee had traveled from Alabama to Aruba with classmates to celebrate their high school graduation, and was due to fly home on May 30, 2005. She was last seen leaving a bar in Oranjestad with van der Sloot and two other young men, brothers Deepak and Satish Kalpoe. All three were questioned and arrested during the investigation, but none was charged in connection with her disappearance at the time.

Natalee’s body has never been found, but van der Sloot has confessed to her murder.

Yes. In 2023, van der Sloot admitted that he killed Natalee in Aruba. According to a transcript of his account, he said they were walking on a beach when she rejected his advances. He said he kicked her and hit her with a cinder block, then pushed her body into the sea.

The confession came as part of a U.S. case against van der Sloot. He had tried to extort money from Natalee's mother, Beth Holloway, by promising to reveal the location of her daughter’s remains and reveal to her what had happened.

He pleaded guilty to extortion and wire fraud and was sentenced to 20 years in prison.

However, van der Sloot was never convicted of Natalee’s murder. The U.S. case was about extortion and wire fraud, and he has not been charged with her killing. So although he confessed, it is more accurate to say he admitted to killing Natalee than to describe him as a convicted murderer (at least in the case of Natalee).

Van der Sloot is in prison in Peru, where he is serving a 28-year sentence for the 2010 murder of Peruvian student Stephany Flores Ramírez. His U.S. sentence runs at the same time as his Peruvian sentence.

As of August 2026, he was reported to be held at Challapalca prison in southern Peru. He had asked to be moved, alleging threats from other inmates; reports said a judge still needed to rule on his request.

Stephany Flores Ramírez was a 21-year-old Peruvian business student. In May 2010, she was found dead in a hotel room in Lima, after checking in with Joran van der Sloot.

Van der Sloot was arrested in Chile and taken back to Peru. He initially confessed to killing Stephany, but later tried to withdraw his confession—however, a court ruled that it could be used as evidence.

In January 2012, he was sentenced to 28 years in prison for her murder and ordered to pay compensation to Stephany’s family.

The timing was especially eerie, as it is believed that Stephany was killed on the fifth anniversary of Natalee's disappearance. Although her case was separate, van der Sloot's arrest put him back in the spotlight and brought fresh attention to the questions that still surrounded Natalee's disappearance.

In 2023, van der Sloot admitted to killing her too, although he has never been convicted of Natalee's murder.

#6759. A Look Inside the 2026 Dia Art Foundation Fall Gala

Attendees included Sofia Coppola, Marion Cotillard, and Matthieu Blazy

Last night marked a busy evening in the world of fashion. In New York City, the 2026 CFDA Fashion Awards were hosted at the American Museum of Natural History, while in Los Angeles, a party was held to celebrate a new book documenting photographer Richard Avedon’s work in Harper’s Bazaar. But that wasn’t all—even more members of the fashion and art worlds united for the Dia Art Foundation’s annual fall gala in NYC.

The event acted as a celebration of two creatives—artist Roni Horn and British film director Steve McQueen—as well as a fundraiser for the arts. Guests gathered at the Dia Chelsea museum before migrating to a cocktail party and dinner where the honorees were toasted.

Some of the starry names in attendance included director Sofia Coppola and Oscar-winning actor Marion Cotillard. This is the first year that the gala has been sponsored by Chanel, after the French fashion house announced its partnership with the non-profit organization back in May. So, this naturally meant that Chanel’s current creative director, Matthieu Blazy, was also present (and he has been teaming up with the organization for years, even since he was at the helm of Bottega Veneta). In 2027, Chanel will sponsor the Dia Beacon gallery’s annual Spring Benefit luncheon, too.

Remarks were shared by the chairman of the Dia Foundation’s board of trustees, Nathalie de Gunzburg; the Dia director, Jessica Morgan; as well as by Matthew Barney, who paid tribute to Horn. Proceeds from the evening will go towards the foundation’s restoration projects, while also contributing to art-related lectures, commissions, educational programs, and more.

Ahead, see all the stars who attended the Dia Art Foundation’s 2026 fall gala.

Michael Stipe, Matthieu Blazy, and Lucy Chadwick

#6758. Colin Jost Takes Gift-Giving Seriously

The Weekend Update co-host and Kay campaign star shares his approach to holiday shopping.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Finding the perfect holiday gift isn’t easy. Between the pressure to get it right, the endless wish lists, and the people who insist they don’t want anything, shopping for loved ones can feel like an impossible mission. Even Colin Jost, who has spent years navigating the pressures of live television, admits that the process is incredibly stressful.

This festive season, the Weekend Update co-host is putting his comedic talents to work with Kay by starring in the jeweler’s latest holiday campaign, “Love All In.” The cheeky ad follows Jost on an increasingly ridiculous quest to find the perfect present, poking fun at the lengths we’ll go to for the people closest to us. It’s a fitting partnership for someone who knows a thing or two about making people laugh but also takes gifting pretty seriously.

“I think I’m a good gift giver,” Jost tells ELLE. “Giving gifts is stressful because I do want it to be a meaningful gift. I hate the idea of just buying something because you have to give someone something.”

For Jost, the secret to getting it right has less to do with how much you spend and more to do with how much thought goes into it. “At every kind of price point for any gift, there [are] thoughtful and unthoughtful versions,” he says. “Something can be really expensive but not thoughtful.”

It’s a sentiment that aligns with Kay’s latest campaign, which encourages shoppers to go “all in” when expressing their love. And while Jost might not be the first person you’d think to turn to for jewelry advice, he’s developed an appreciation for it over the years, thanks to his very chic wife, Scarlett Johansson.

“Until five years ago, I didn’t own a watch,” he admits. That changed when the actress gifted him his first one, introducing him to an entirely new world of accessories. As for whether she’s influenced his personal style? “In that it’s exclusively managed by her, yes, I would say she’s influenced it.”

Despite his Staten Island roots, Jost’s jewelry preferences are surprisingly understated. “I don’t really wear a ton of jewelry, but I also am from Staten Island, so I do have to have certain pieces, or else they won’t let me back over the Verrazano Bridge,” he jokes.

While browsing Kay’s assortment, he found himself drawn to simple gold pieces, including a ring, bracelet, and necklace. “They were really nice, but also they weren’t gaudy or anything,” he says. His preference for gold also comes with some rather unconventional reasoning: “If we get into a post-apocalyptic situation, it feels like it’s better to melt down for trading.”

When it comes to shopping for the people closest to him, Jost admits that both Johansson and his Weekend Update collaborator Michael Che are difficult to buy things for, though he found a creative solution for the latter. “I got Che a really large and heavy, realistic statue of a velociraptor,” he recalls. “It was so heavy that he literally can’t get rid of it.” The real expense wasn’t the dinosaur itself. “The main cost was shipping,” he says. “It was a huge eyesore, and it’s prohibitively expensive for him to have removed from his property.”

The hardest person to gift on Jost’s list? His father, who insists he doesn’t need anything. “I think he actually means it, but it’s so unhelpful,” Jost says. After years of nostalgic gifts inspired by his dad’s past, he’s running out of ideas. “I’ve got to figure out something that breaks through his defenses.”

In terms of gifting etiquette, Jost has a few rules of his own. For starters, he’s big on surprises. While he understands the appeal of a wish list for children, he thinks adults should leave a little room for the unexpected. “For adults, it’s got to be ‘surprise me,’” he says. “The surprise is half the fun, three-quarters of the fun.” Regifting, on the other hand, comes with a few conditions. He’s open to the idea, but only if the gift is a perfect fit for someone and there’s full disclosure.

But perhaps the most surprising revelation comes when I ask what’s on his own holiday wish list. After spending much of our conversation discussing what to buy everyone else, Jost seems relieved to finally be on the receiving end. “Oh, thank you! Finally, someone asked me. What do I want?”

His answer? Nothing material. “At this point in life, I mostly want experiences,” he says. Whether it’s a trip or simply someone taking the initiative to organize an outing, he’s more interested in making memories than collecting more things. “Even if it’s just the plan of, ‘Let’s go do this at this time,’ I think that’s a great gift.” Or, when in doubt, a velociraptor.

#6757. A '90s Prada Obsessive Picks Her Four Favorite Collections

To know Laia Garcia-Furtado is to understand that she’s obsessed with Prada. Indeed, it’s one of her most obvious traits. During her tenure as senior fashion news editor here at Vogue, she spoke at length about her expertise on Miuccia Prada on our podcast, “The Run-Through,” and wrote countless odes to her beloved brand. So when she was approached by an editor at Batsford Books with a proposal to write a book on Prada, it felt preordained. “I feel like I’ve been training my entire life for this,” she said. Still, as an editor herself, Garcia-Furtado had to gut-check herself. “I quickly remembered that there’s a lot more to ’90s Prada than those legendary collections that came in the second half of the decade.”

Her new book Prada in the Nineties comes at a great time for the new generation of Miuccia-heads, discovering the brand on social media. “I think everyone is still so obsessed with the ’90s because, looking back, we think of it as the last decade of a kind of freedom,” Garcia-Furtado explains. “The Internet was barely beginning, and people still dressed to their own spirit. But I think for Prada, especially, this is the decade where it went from a kind of fledgling label to the global behemoth it is today.” She adds that it was during this period that Mrs. Prada discovered her voice and encompassing vision for what the brand would one day become. “All those ’90s collections are still constantly referenced to this day.”

Even as a devotee, the research process yielded a treasure trove of information about Mrs. Prada, like the fact that the designer’s spring 1999 collection was actually her way of fighting back against the trends of the ’90s (trends, of course, she helped popularize). “The spring 1999 collection with mirror embellishments was her sort of ‘taking the piss’ to the hippie trend that was happening around the time,” says Garcia-Furtado. “I love when you see her humor peeking through, and that really reminded me of the lace collection from fall 2008—another one of my all-time favorites—that she said she made because she hated lace.”

Because the book is unauthorized and unapproved by Prada—as she wittily notes on her cover—Garcia-Furtado tapped illustrator Rachel Song to reimagine her favorite collections (think a chinoiserie look on page 133 or the spring 1999 bumbag on page 167). When asked if she’s sent a copy to Prada headquarters yet, she had a pithy reply: “You can’t just reach out to God like that.” Here’s to hoping a copy makes its way to Milan regardless.

On the occasion of the book being published, we asked Garcia-Furtado to share her four favorite ’90s Prada collections, and explain what makes them so special.

“I think spring 1994 is maybe an underrated collection, or maybe it was underrated to me, and now I love it. The strange sheer nightgowns layered over knitted separates and worn with little white socks and odd moccasins. The sudden Renaissance influence with the leather vests over the dreamy white dresses. It really captured the grunge spirit of the time yet in a purely Miuccia way, but I think it’s still really modern.”

“Spring 1996 is maybe my all-time favorite fashion collection, the root cause of my personal style. The mismatched prints, all the different shades of green that are supposed to be ugly but couldn’t possibly be. (It’s worth noting that my teen girl bedroom had two walls painted bright slime green and two opposing walls painted mint green—so I wouldn’t go totally crazy according to my mom.) But even beyond the prints, the silhouettes are pure Prada: a midi-length skirt ending in the no man’s land between knee and ankle, a simple slip dress worn under a car coat, strange sandals that expose entirely too much toe. Everything feels a little bit off, but it’s also perfectly feminine, perfectly balanced.”

“This was the season when Mrs. Prada started thinking about the future, like suddenly the idea of the new millennium became real. Everything looks a little bit tech-y yet also romantic, as if it came from a near-distant past. Beads look like the inside of a computer or maybe drops of rain? Her favorite little button-down shirts now come with latex insets. I also love the berry color palette, both in the ladylike suits with oddly-gathered skirts, and on the lips! Also, this season had those divine pink lucite heels that are definitely at the top of my wish list.”

“The early ’90s collections were my blind spot going into this project, but the more time I spent with some of them, the more I found things I loved. The clothes are deceptively freaky. I love Naomi’s loose-leaf bright white satin tunic and matching cigarette trousers, an odd red and white vertical striped dress with strangely sculptural shoulder lines. But my favorite look is Carla Bruni in a bike short twinset complete with pillbox hat. It’s so prim it’s almost totally demented.”

Prada in the Nineties will be available on November 10, 2026.

#6753. Forget Arrakis, Zendaya’s Latest Look Comes From a London Auto Shop

If Ronan McKenzie called her spring 2027 collection for Selasi “Going Places,” this week it did just that when Law Roach enlisted the designer to provide clothes for Zendaya. (The image architect has been keeping himself pretty busy with his other very famous client, Kim Kardashian.)

Earlier this week, Zendaya appeared alongside her co-star Timothée Chalamet in a promotional video for Dune: Part Three, wearing look three from the show: a sleeveless halter-neck top and barrel-legged trousers in sandy shades of suede evocative of the movie’s desert landscape.

It’s always a thrill to see how far a collection can travel beyond the runway, particularly when it comes from a label with relatively modest resources. This was only McKenzie’s second runway presentation since joining the London Fashion Week schedule, staged in a mechanic’s garage and modeled by the designer’s inner circle, among them her mum, British Vogue contributing editor Zezi Ifore and entrepreneur Charlene Prempeh.

“Their presence underscored a central idea behind the collection: these clothes are intended to live beyond the runway,” read the show notes. “Going Places is grounded in pieces that can move through different bodies, ages, and lives.” Another headline-making moment from Selasi’s recent runways was the rediscovery of 59-year-old model Evelyn Effrim-Botchey, who has since gone on to make waves on Paris’s most rarefied catwalks.

Of course, Roach could commission custom looks from any number of luxury fashion houses—he has even been known to charter private jets to ensure Zendaya is dressed in just-seen-on-the-runway haute couture gowns—but he has made a concerted effort to champion emerging designers, too. The previous Dune press tour saw the actor wear Torishéju and Paolo Carzana, while she was an early supporter of Peter Do and Maximilian Davis’s Ferragamo before that. For a young designer, a co-sign of this caliber can make all the difference, offering a way through an industry difficult to break into.

“The energy you put into something won’t always lead to the outcome you imagine,” McKenzie told Vogue not long ago. “But you still have to fight, keep going, restart and pick yourself up off the ground.” It’s paying off.

#6752. Kate Moss Proves the Leather Midi Skirt Is Eternally Appealing

Moss has always got a new way to make a classic item feel fresh.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Kate Moss is era-defining—as synonymous with the look of Cool Britannia in the ’90s as she is with Indie Sleaze. And yet, when you take apart Moss’s wardrobe, it’s clear to see that the supermodel has always stuck to the classics. Case in point: her look to attend Tom Ford’s SS27 show during Paris Fashion Week on September 30.

The 52-year-old arrived in support of her friend and the brand’s creative director, Haider Ackermann, wearing a tried-and-tested formula of a button-down Oxford shirt and sleek and shiny leather pencil skirt. Her take was loaded with sex appeal and timeless allure in equal measure.

The brilliance of Moss’s look came in its application. She’s always been great at styling familiar pieces in inventive ways to bring them back into rotation. Here, she left her shirt open entirely and rolled up the sleeves, with the high waist of the skirt stepping in at just the right point. Her choice of a string bra kept things feeling more contemporary than the more obvious choice of something lacy, perhaps. Sunglasses for the nighttime added A-list appeal, while her green stone ring reminded us of her often bohemian style sensibility. The final touch of strappy sandals finished Moss’s look with the sky-high heels she often turns to.

The cut of the skirt was key, too. Its pencil shape added to that classic ideal of sex-appeal dressing, feeling typically true of Tom Ford’s design signature that Ackermann now does his own way. It’s a shape that never goes out of style, whether it’s worn like Moss or in contrast with a roomy wool jumper or a lacy camisole.

It’s the skirt of the season. How will you wear yours?

#6750. Meet Melat Kiros, the 29-Year-Old Bringing Gen Z Politics to Congress

After losing her law-firm job over her stance on Gaza protests, she turned generational frustration into a historic win.

In 2023, Melat Kiros was fired. She was 26 and worked at a major New York law firm, but she refused to condemn an open letter addressed to dozens of law firms that had urged law school deans to crack down on antisemitism on university campuses, at a time when students were protesting the slaughter in Gaza. Kiros condemned antisemitism, but warned law firms, including the one she worked for, against confusing “bigotry with the geopolitical question of Israel’s legitimacy.” This cost her her job.

She, however, was not discouraged, as she belongs to a generation that is no longer willing to set aside its values for the sake of a career. “The right to question not only the legitimacy of a government but also its actions is the bedrock principle of a true democracy,” she said of her decision. Since then, Kiros has devoted herself more intensely to politics, and yesterday, in the midterm elections, she won as Colorado’s representative to the House of Representatives. Before her, only one other Gen Z representative, Democrat Maxwell Frost of Florida, had made it to Parliament in 2022, but she became the first Gen Z woman in Congress.

Melat Kiros was born in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia, but moved to Denver with her family when she was still a baby and grew up there. She graduated from Washington College in Maryland in 2018, where she studied political science and economics, before earning a law degree from the University of Notre Dame in 2022. Today she is a lawyer and is pursuing a doctorate in public policy. In the meantime, however, she also works as a barista. It is not easy to balance a political career with her studies, but it is worth it.

After being fired, Kiros returned to Colorado and, in 2024, joined the election campaign of Democratic congressional candidate John Padora as communications director. It was excellent training for her, so much so that, two years later, she decided to run for Congress herself. On her side, she has strong political positions—she supports universal health care and child care, but also more politically controversial issues, such as abolishing Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) and ending U.S. military aid to Israel. Her ideals openly align with her actions, and in fact during the election campaign she refused funding from corporate PACs and pro-Israel groups, while also sharply criticizing her Democratic primary opponent Diana DeGette, who had instead accepted donations from defense contractors as well as energy and pharmaceutical companies.

It is clear that Kiros's mark is different: She shows us what Gen Z can and will be able to do when it is given space. In this sense, her election also fits well into the climate of student protests, in Europe and around the world, by a generation that wants to make its voice heard. Yet it is still not easy: According to research by Future Caucus, more than 100 Generation Z candidates ran in the 2026 midterm elections for Congress, but the study found that young candidates were less likely to make it to the general election than other age groups, with only 24 of them appearing on the November ballots.

Kiros also had to face paternalism and dismissive attitudes from those who considered her unsuitable and inexperienced. “Now is not the time to gamble and send somebody with no experience to Washington,” DeGette had said during the primaries. Kiros, however, turned it into a strength, centering her congressional candidacy precisely on the theme of generational change. She emphasized socialist issues dear to younger people, such as affordability, social policies, climate change, countering the influence of multinational corporations, and support for students, families, and workers.

“People, I think, were really eager to see someone who is younger, who has lived experience with the economy that we're living through right now, the climate crisis that we are going to have to deal with the consequences of,” she said in an interview. For her, “socialism is about making it clear that we have basic needs for every single human being,” and she has promised to fight for this.

She also knows she has a lot to learn, but that does not stop her from walking through the doors of Congress with her head held high, without being overly intimidated by traditions and alliances. “Young people understand that we are in the fight of our lives,” she said. “We need to have leaders that are going to be fighting for the kind of bold policies that actually get something done, and they’re refusing to settle or wait for our turn or ask for permission.”

#6749. Jennifer Lawrence’s Novelty T-Shirt Is an Archive Pull for the Ages—and All Ages

Jennifer Lawrence has a soft spot for a novelty item. Vintage Issey Miyake cats, Twilight merch, a litany of slogan-topped baseball caps have all found their way into the actor’s wardrobe. Her latest addition, however, is a particularly nostalgic one. A fashion archive pull for all ages, if you will.

Spotted out in New York City on Tuesday, the actor and now beauty brand founder leaned into her always colorful approach to off-duty dressing. The actor wore a white Sesame Street tee emblazoned with a scribbly blue portrait of Ernie, which she paired with slouchy, tomato-red Freecity Airjump pants, a blue-and-red plaid shirt knotted around her waist, and an oversized cobalt Auralee canvas jacket with a dark shearling-style collar. On her feet, a pair of lack-and-white Vans Authentic sneakers, and she added some small oval sunglasses. Her blonde hair was pulled into a loose and laidback braid—a little Katniss Everdeen-esque. There was another throwback in the mix, too. Lawrence flashed the three-finger salute made famous by her Hunger Games character for the cameras.

Lawrence’s fall wardrobe has been an ongoing exercise in getting comfortable without sacrificing character. Just last week, she took a sumptuous, floor-sweeping chartreuse house coat by New York designer Colleen Allen out for a spin, layering it over a lime Miu Miu cardigan and finishing the look with fuzzy black slippers. Elsewhere, there have been oversized knits, louche trousers, baseball caps, and all manner of unexpected color combinations. Cozy season is literal in the Lawrence household.

Lawrence’s more relaxed street style is often offset with her considered fashion eye. She might favor the quiet luxury of The Row, Khaite, and Phoebe Philo, but she’s just as likely to throw an irreverent graphic tee, a kooky accessory, or a flash of acid-bright color into the mix. Her wardrobe is expensive but never overly precious. And while her latest outfit might look like something borrowed from a pretty well-dressed child’s wardrobe, Jennifer Lawrence knows that approaching low-key looks require a certain beat of unseriousness—care of Elmo, et al, or otherwise.

#6748. Statement Scarves Are Fall’s Defining Accessory

Puffed, twisted, and totally oversize: Scarves are taking over as new statements.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The behemoth sculptural statement scarf is here, and it’s never looked better. Puffed, twisted, oversize, and opulent, these finishing pieces eclipse the coat and become a one-and-done look for winter weather. These are not your usual blanket scarves or faux fur creations. The new statement scarf is padded and sculpted, often done in fabrics like luscious leather. These XL scarves were seen on the fall/winter 2026 runway at Loewe, Celine, and Toteme. Elsewhere, Givenchy showcased the power of a smaller leather scarf done in brilliant cobalt blue. The main message with all of these outfit-defining scarves is that they do all the talking and create a strong visual impact with even the most simple of coats. Black leather jackets, neutral wool coats, basic blazers, and even beige trenches get a new look with a dramatic scarf. It’s no surprise the look is trending now. After season upon season of quiet luxury, we’re now firmly in the era of the statement coat.

But for those who don’t want to fully commit to the look, we have the statement scarf, which gives instant drama with ease. If you’re in need of styling inspiration, follow the runways’ lead. Keep it tonal and choose a scarf in the same color palette as your coat or jacket, or go the opposite way and pick up a primary hue like scarlet red or bright yellow to add contrast to a more subdued-hued jacket.

There’s also something beautifully haphazard about these pieces: On the runway, many of them are intentionally styled so that they look windswept or as if they were in motion and got frozen mid-moment. For designers like Michael Rider at Celine, that was part of it. The label’s fall/winter 2026 show notes presented the collection as “intuition over strategy. Feeling it rather than planning it,” and “contradiction, imperfection, character.” Adding to this, “I love when messy, complex, layered inner lives come through underneath great clothes,” Rider expressed in the show notes. “Thinking about people with style who wear beautiful clothes in a personal way.”

On a more practical level, the statement scarf adds a little bit of pizzazz with warmth. It acts as wearable sculpture. Tied tightly around the neck, styled with an over-the-top pair of sunglasses, it brings the focus to the face and creates an iconic look that is also strangely practical.

We’re all so bundled under layers in these cooler months that accessorizing (and wearing jewelry) can pose a challenge. Many of the big puffy scarves are editorial showpieces that may not have made it to the retail side of fashion, but the other side of statement scarves lies in embellishment. Think: embroidered wonders from Dries Van Noten, paillette triangle scarves from Zankov, or even just a classic scarf done with extra-long fringe. Just think of it as a warm version of a necklace.

#6745. Reckoning With Borat, 20 Years Later

Last month, I sat in a packed-to-the-gills movie theater to experience the latest release from a controversial comedian whose work regularly blurs the line between docu-reality and narrative fiction. I am, of course, referring to Ali G: Who Iz I?—the cinematic return of Sacha Baron Cohen’s once-and-still-somewhat-beloved durag-doffing titular character.

Before the film was unceremoniously dumped on Prime Video last week, I managed to snag a ticket to the only New York City screening that wasn’t totally sold-out, booking a seat—last row, tucked in an alcove of the theater—with the intention of isolated note-taking and (gasp!) the occasional phone-glance should the movie prove tedious. The recliners next to me were eventually claimed by a gentleman decked in a very Ali G-esque skull cap and wraparounds, who unleashed a deafening “BOOYAKASHA!” after Nicole Kidman’s AMC intro.

Cohen’s brand of confrontational cringe comedy continues to attract a level of interest, thanks in part to a certain mustached-and-mankini’d Kazakh journalist. The cultural explosion that was Borat! Cultural Learnings of America for Make Benefit Glorious Nation of Kazakhstan (has any movie title so padded the word count?) turns 20 years old this week, and it remains a curious artifact of the 2000s: impossibly dated and slightly better than you might remember, even as its creator’s talent has slowly and unmistakably eroded.

If you were just old enough to purchase a ticket to an R-rated movie in 2006, Borat felt like a truly out-of-nowhere phenomenon in terms of mass impact. But the character, armed with little more than a shabby suit and an aggressive cheer, emerged from Cohen’s cult favorite Da Ali G Show, which ran for one season on BBC’s Channel 4 in 2000 before making the leap to HBO in 2003. Alongside Ali G and the gay Austrian fashion correspondent Brüno, Borat Sagdiyev was just one of the many personalities Cohen regularly deployed in the thorny thickets of gotcha-mentary, trading in culturally oblivious and antisemitic observations supposedly inherited from his Kazakh homeland, often to the shock and dismay of his unsuspecting subjects.

These fool-me-once hijinks weren’t necessarily groundbreaking, even for HBO’s then-provocative late-night programming. Several years prior, The Chris Rock Show engaged in similar man-on-the-street shenanigans (once, he polled residents of NYC’s notoriously hate-crime-prone Howard Beach region about renaming a street after Tupac Shakur). But segments in which Ali G addressed Buzz Aldrin as Buzz Lightyear, or the one where Borat led a bar full of Arizonans in a rousing chorus of “Throw the Jew Down the Well,” were practically reverse-engineered for virality in the internet’s pre-social-media infancy, and the appeal of Cohen’s approach—how far will he go, and will ordinary folk follow him?—was easy to understand, regardless of the visceral discomfort it may have generated.

Straying from the sketch-based format of Da Ali G Show, Borat embraced a loose narrative structure, with its titular inquirer setting out for America to make a documentary on the country’s social peccadilloes with the intent of eventually romancing Pamela Anderson. If you want to get highfalutin about it, the plot itself situates Borat within the legacy of films about Europeans attempting to understand America—a small-but-fascinating subgenre that includes Paul Verhoeven’s fairytale-cum-nightmare Showgirls, the post-hippie pessimism of Michaelangelo Antonioni’s Zabriskie Point, and Kristoffer Borgli’s social-mores rom-dramedy The Drama.

Don’t get it twisted, though: Borat was and still is best enjoyed as a showcase of physical comedy and inflammatory non sequiturs. It’s no less funny in 2026 to watch a grown man wash his face in a toilet bowl, or gesture toward a tortoise while sincerely asking, “What type of dog is this?” The scene in which an etiquette coach attempts to guide Borat through the intricacies of comic timing is itself an excellent study in comic timing; the notorious full-frontal hotel chase sequence between Borat and his producer Azamat (portrayed by steadily-working character actor Ken Davitian) is a marvel for its sheer escalation alone. This stuff killed in a massive Northern New Jersey movie theater when I was 19, and it still kills from the confines of my couch 20 years on.

Unlike much of Cohen’s subsequent work, Borat is not an explicitly political film. Nonetheless, a good deal of its “GREAT SUCCESS!”—$262 million in box receipts off an $18 million budget, with a Best Actor Golden Globe for Cohen himself—is undoubtedly owed to a different sort of comic timing. The film came out in the throes of George W. Bush’s second presidential term, with the US mired in two wars abroad and a hell of its own making. Americans were thirsty for easy targets to shoulder the blame and, if possible, provide some cheap laughs in the process.

Their thirst was quenched in the form of Borat’s many unsuspecting subjects. Some of this was low-hanging fruit: Borat telling a group of feminists to smile more, or acting like a literal bull in a china shop amidst Confederate memorabilia. But the film’s more sensational material depicted rough corners of the American psyche long hidden in plain sight. Most memorably, Borat encounters a group of rowdy frat boys who huff about white pride and express dismay at the abolishment of slavery before engaging in a viewing of Anderson’s infamous leaked sex tape with ex-husband Tommy Lee.

Not everyone was displeased at their portrayal, though. Joe and Miriam Behar, the Jewish couple who offered Borat and Azamat room and board, were good-natured in a 2006 interview with the Boston Globe. For her part, Miriam shrugged off Borat’s naked antisemitism. “To me it's an anti-Muslim movie, not anti-Jewish, because Kazakhstan is mostly Muslim,” she offered.

Behar’s observation underlines the casual bigotry that otherwise stains Borat. The film’s portrayal of Kazakh culture is undoubtedly offensive, and provoked mixed reactions from the country’s government for years to come. For what it’s worth, Cohen pushes back—intentionally or not—on anti-Muslim sentiments during the film’s rodeo sequence, in which Borat tells a particularly racist attendee that he is indeed “not Muslim.”

But given the post-9/11 climate into which Borat was launched, the implications of a foreign correspondent from a majority-Muslim country espousing antisemitic and misogynistic viewpoints are crystal-clear, and Cohen himself seems too smart to sincerely profess a lack of awareness as to how this might be received. It doesn’t help that his subsequent projects resulted in exponential escalations of offense: 2012’s more narratively-focused The Dictator spared no quarter when it came to stereotypes of Arab culture, while the astoundingly homophobic Brüno, from 2009, was loaded with gags about anal sex and resulted in an out-of-court settlement with a Palestinian grocer who was falsely credited as a former terrorist in the film itself.

After 2016’s box-office bomb Grimsby, which is most notable for the allegations of on-set sexual harassment co-star Rebel Wilson later brought toward Cohen, his work went in a more explicitly political direction. There was his 2018 docu-satire series Who Is America?—two episodes of which featured writing and directing contributions from Nathan Fielder—and 2020’s Borat Subsequent Moviefilm, a second-verse-same-as-the-first sequel which earned several Oscar nominations and brought a truly bizarre “October surprise” to that year’s presidential election involving actress Maria Bakalova, Rudy Giuliani, and the former New York City Mayor’s pants zipper.

Both of those films were more explicitly message-y than they were riotously funny. Cohen’s latest, Ali G: Who Iz I?, is neither, carrying the all-too-familiar stink of a comedian insisting that their capacity to offend amounts to protected speech. At 55, Cohen is clearly not as willing to submit himself to the ragdoll physics he once deployed; in its place is an often-sullen-looking Ali G, his dreads now gray, weighing the merits of cultural appropriation and sparring with a DEI coordinator about the origins of the durag. The entire affair is belabored and depressing.

It made me think of the Who Is America? character Dr. Nira Cain-N'Degeocello, a send-up of Rachel Dolezal-flavored liberals in the form of a Berkeley professor who dons dashikis and keffiyehs. Early in the film, he rages against Ali G’s culturally appropriative ways to a majority-Black community meeting in Oakland. “We don’t care!,” shouts one participant. In hindsight, it’s as much an expression of exhaustion with the dictates of capital-D discourse as it is a referendum on Cohen’s confrontational method, which now yields far less interesting insights about the national psyche than it did when Borat arrived on American soil in 2006.

#6742. Why Did Barry Keoghan Get XERF?

Well, the answer is very simple, according to his facialist.

In the past week, everyone seems to have discovered XERF. A radiofrequency treatment originally from Korea, it can tighten, tone, and lift skin, and it’s now becoming a verb. Kim Kardashian got XERFed. So did Priyanka Chopra, Martha Stewart, Cardi B, and also Barry Keoghan. Out of all the celebrities who have gotten it, it’s Keoghan who has made the most headlines.

His longtime facialist, Nilam Holmes, posted a video of Keoghan receiving XERF while he says in a voice-over that “It’s very relaxing and therapeutic. I feel better about myself; I look better. I think it’s important to look after yourself inside and outside.” It’s so unusual to see an actor, let alone a male actor, star in a beauty video that ELLE called up Holmes, a U.K.-based facialist whose clients include Rosie Huntington-Whiteley (whom she prepped for the Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show) and Joe Alwyn, to learn more about red carpet protocols, how Keoghan got XERFed, and what she thinks of all the press attention.

XERF only just launched in the U.K. nine weeks ago, and there was a waiting list. Everyone’s been waiting so long to have it. Celebrities are sharing their experiences, which doesn’t usually happen with a lot of treatments. People have also been traveling to Korea to have it done and then going back to the U.S. to talk about it.

I invest in a lot of the top-of-the-line devices and use really reputable machines. Currently, I have things like Ultraformer III, Ultherapy, and Morpheus8. Most of them have some downtime, or they’re really painful—you’re breaking the skin and using heat. People can look like they have grid marks or are red for a bit.

XERF has no downtime and is not painful, so you don’t need numbing cream. I wouldn’t say it’s pain-free, but it’s not painful. I could do one now and somebody could go out tonight and be fine. You’re getting a big result quickly that carries on working, and it’s an alternative to those other treatments. It is probably one of the pricier things that I offer in my clinic. It’s a bit misleading when they say nonsurgical facelift. It’s more of a skin-tightening treatment.

When I did it on myself, I did half of my face, and I could see a considerable difference. One of the first XERF treatments that I did on a celebrity was on Nicole Scherzinger, and midway through, I ran out of the special gas that goes in the cooler and half of her face looked so different. It does give quite a dramatic, instant result.

People who don’t need a lot of lift get the best results. You see contours better. It gives a bit of a Cinderella effect, so the instant result looks better than it will look three days later. About 50 percent of people have come back and said it was great; they want a second session, and then others are like, “Yeah, it’s good, but I still think Morpheus was better.” You notice the most difference after one treatment. It’s quite a pricey treatment. I’ve not had anybody say they don’t notice a difference.

I do a lot of celebrities. The sort of higher up you go, the less you can mention. But what happened with Barry? Bless him. I did a lady here in the U.K. who is a big celebrity and normally every time she does something, the press picks it up, but this time, they didn’t; they picked Barry’s over hers. The press was very random. He does come in regularly. We don’t do anything crazy. He had his first XERF and was having his second one.

On the day he was coming in, I was meant to be filming my own face. He came really early and I was running really late and had said, “Barry, could you come back later? I’ve got to film myself having it.” He said, “I’ll do it for you,” and that was it. It wasn’t a setup. It wasn’t an endorsement. He just did it for me. The press was a little bit unfair—they said he was having it because trolls claimed he was rushing to have a treatment to change his look. Barry likes his looks. His commentary wasn’t, I look terrible. I don’t feel confident to go out. I don’t think that was the case at all.

XERF doesn’t have the ability to change anybody’s face, and a lot of the other treatments don’t either. Surgery or excessive fillers are the only things that change people’s faces. But he’s filming a movie and doing long hours. He does get a tired look on his skin. He likes to just do some things that give a little bit of a pick-me-up, and XERF does that. For poor Barry, people were saying, “I used to like you, and now I think you’re weak because you’re having something [done].” Some of the comments are like that. Well, it’s only a skin treatment.

Some old pictures from earlier this year were about his skin texture. We’ve done a little bit of micro-needling, and he has Hydrafacials, which have helped his texture a lot. It also helps when you’re lying down under good lighting as well. I’ve been looking after Barry for a while. We did him for Peaky Blinders. His skin looked great in that, but at the minute, he’s on a movie where they use prosthetic makeup and that doesn’t help the skin at all.

It’s not unusual for the guys to come in with bits of glue left from prosthetics and then that peels the skin a little bit as well. I’ve seen Barry since, and he’s fine. His agents were more unhappy about him doing that. He wasn’t saying that people should go and have those treatments.

But why shouldn’t men do skin treatments? At the moment, I have a higher percentage of male clients in my own clinic. And it’s not just actors, footballers, or pop stars. I have barristers, doctors, and high-flying professionals. [For actors], makeup ruins the skin, and they start looking more and more tired on camera. Most people do have some sort of facial when they’re on a movie and definitely before a red carpet. Now it’s the norm for everybody to have a facial prior to grooming.

I’m in London, but it’s got a very discreet and private entrance. Men realize when they come in that it’s actually a really nice experience. Men like the machines—the radiofrequency, the Hydrafacial—and they’re not into the fluffy lymphatic drainage massage. They want the tech. Once they see the difference, they’re very loyal. They don’t want to go and try somewhere else.

I don’t think it’s about that everyone’s trying to look younger. People are just trying to stay as they are. The longer you stay looking like that, the more parts you’re going to get. I’ve got actors in their eighties that still come.

Men are shamed into thinking it’s vanity or almost like, you’re defeated if you had something done. I don’t think that’s a nice message because we all want to look nice. We want to be comfortable with what we see in the mirror. I try to encourage that and take the shame out of it because it shouldn’t exist. What’s wrong with having a facial, a little collagen-boosting treatment, or micro-needling, which is one of the oldest, most organic ones you can have?

My attitude is that the firmer and healthier the skin, the more glowy it looks. Your skin doesn’t have to be wrinkle-free, but when you’ve got a bit of bounce and radiance, that makes you look younger. It doesn’t have to be stretched tight. It’s not preventing aging, but just slowing it down.

For two days, I would do a radiofrequency skin-tightening treatment—maybe not more than a Collagenwave or Exion treatment, because it will give you firmness and contour the face. Add a Hydrafacial to clean up the skin. Then, I would infuse some exosomes or a skin booster via a tiny micro-needling stamp. It basically gets into the surface of the skin. I put them under a red light. I just do some Dermalux light afterwards. If somebody wants to do something extra, EmFace is always good to add in because that helps to tone the muscles as well.

You could do it. Kim Kardashian did say, “This is my red carpet treatment.” Firstly, I think it’s too long a treatment to do for that. It’s also very expensive, too. But if I was getting someone ready for the Oscars, our prep would start a month before. Then, I would do something like XERF or micro-needling. I micro-needle with hyper-diluted Sculptra or exosomes. That takes about a month to get a full result. And I would also do dermaplaning, which takes all the peach fuzz away. When you put your makeup on, it reflects, rather than the hair absorbing the light.

I love a good eyebrow—we do Korean lamination. It softens the hairs of the brow or the lashes. It makes them more polished-looking, so that they fit really nicely and you can brush them up or across, making them look fuller so you can get a better shape out of them. I could do a skin peel in 10 minutes for somebody if they don’t have time, and then they can go straight into glam or grooming.

This interview has been condensed and edited for clarity.

#6741. Dior and Harper’s Bazaar Host a Screening of the New Richard Avedon Documentary in Los Angeles

With Emma Watson, Gia Coppola, and more in attendance

From a Paris Fashion Week toast to a New York City launch party, Dior and Harper’s Bazaar have been honoring the legacy of fashion photographer Richard Avedon these past few weeks. This series of intimate events has been tied to the launch of a new coffee-table book titled Richard Avedon: The Harper’s Bazaar Years, as well as a documentary film, Avedon. Now, the festivities have traveled to Los Angeles.

Avedon, who passed away in 2004 at the age of 81, was one of the most influential fashion photographers of the 20th century. In 1944, at just 21 years old, he started his career at Harper’s Bazaar under the guidance of then-editor-in-chief Carmel Snow, and he spent the next two-plus decades defining the creative vision of the publication.

Richard Avedon: The Harper’s Bazaar Years (available now) explores this artistic period by compiling rarely seen photographs, a never-before-published account from Avedon himself, a foreword by editor-in-chief Samira Nasr, and an essay by Bazaar contributing editor Derek C. Blasberg. The new documentary, meanwhile, offers a look through the Avedon archives from a different lens. It was directed by Academy Award–winning filmmaker Ron Howard (Apollo 13, A Beautiful Mind, The Da Vinci Code) and produced by Imagine Documentaries. It will be released on Netflix on November 11.

Focusing on the film, Dior and Blasberg hosted a private screening of Avedon at the William Morris Endeavor headquarters in L.A. Guests such as Emma Watson, Gia Coppola, Lauren Hutton, Devon Lee Carlson, and more gathered to watch the film before heading to the House of Dior in Beverly Hills for a cocktail reception.

Ahead, we offer an intimate look inside the event.

#6734. Why El Clasico Is the Best Grudge Match in All of Sports

Last Saturday, before the biggest grudge match in international soccer, Spanish police officers decked out in riot gear surrounded my Barcelona hotel. Blue lights bounced from the sun-stained stone of the alley as kids scrambled away in the distance. In the skirmish, one had lost a single ASICS sneaker that sat idly in the road until, sometime between night and the light of day, someone snatched it up for themselves. You often hear of the way heated football rivalries and cup finals bring out a kind of mob mentality among fans. The match, however, wasn’t set to kick off for another 24 hours.

Three days prior I’d landed in Barcelona to attend FC Barcelona versus Real Madrid, better known as El Clásico, for which Tyler the Creator’s GOLF brand had partnered with Spotify on Barcelona’s gameday kits. If you care about football in the slightest you know about El Clásico, though it’s worth knowing about even if you don’t.

Since 1902—the year of Real Madrid’s founding, and three years after that of FC Barcelona—the two biggest football clubs in Spain have clashed in a rivalry match dubbed El Clásico (the face-off now happens twice a season, plus various tournament play that varies in schedule year to year). Before last week’s installment, the match had been played 264 times over 122 years. In most high-stakes football rivalries, one club holds a historical edge: Arsenal has a handy 20 to 30 wins over Tottenham Hotspur in the North London Derby, depending on when you start keeping track, while Manchester United edges out Liverpool in total wins. But heading into the first El Clásico of the 2026-27 La Liga season, Barcelona and Real Madrid stood deadlocked with 106 wins each.

American sports rivalries are often the result of proximity, with most pitting divisional neighbors and crosstown rivals against one another, with the exception of teams that share a bitter history of championship bouts (like the Lakers and the Celtics). But if we’re being honest, not even Yankees-Red Sox holds a candle to El Clásico. Apart from both competing in the upper echelon of La Liga’s clubs, FC Barcelona and Real Madrid’s rivalry has stewed in over a century of political turmoil between the Catalan capital and the city that houses the Spanish throne. During the 20th century as Spain looked to enforce a uniform identity across the nation, Catalonia sought to maintain its independence from the crown and, crucially, its right to speak Catalan rather than Spanish. Francisco Franco’s dictatorship brought more enmity, as Madrid became the proxy club for a centralized Spain while Barcelona came to represent Catalonia’s Republican Left. During the Franco years, when both the language and the flag of Catalan were banned, Barcelona’s Camp Nou stadium became a safe haven for the free expression of Catalan identity. Meanwhile, Franco’s foreign minister Fernando Maria de Castiella declared Real Madrid “the best embassy we’ve ever had.” The wounds of that era have hardly subsided: while the war may be over, as recently as 2017 the Catalan people voted on whether or not they’d remain a part of Spain (the vote was declared illegal by the Spanish government, and there was police intervention at polling stations).

Today, the clubs are recognized as polar opposites in every sense, from politics to style of play: Real Madrid stands for conservatism and a unified Spain, while Barcelona remains the club of the rebellious Left. Madrid takes an Evil Empire approach to roster construction, buying up the most exciting free agents on the market for record-breaking fees. (This century alone they’ve rostered Cristiano Ronaldo, David Beckham, and Zinedine Zidane, and the club currently boasts a striker line that includes Jude Bellingham, Kylian Mbappé, and Vinicius Jr.) Barcelona, on the other hand, has the most acclaimed and successful youth academy in all of football. The school, La Masia, has promoted a singular theory of football for decades, prioritizing position play and a dominant possession game perfected by FC Barcelona veterans like Lionel Messi, Xavi, Pep Guardiola, and Andrés Iniesta. When Spain won the World Cup this summer, they did so with eight former students of La Masia on the pitch (one of whom, Marc Cucarella, currently plays for Real Madrid).

To this day, the rivalry is characterized by an unmistakable hostility. In my four days in Barcelona, I didn’t see one person wearing Real Madrid’s crest, though the souvenir shops lining La Rambla were well-stocked with the club’s bootleg gear. At one point, I walked past a wall scrawled with graffiti: “SPANISH GO HOME.” When we finally got to the match, most Madrid fans were sequestered to a supporter section in the upper deck surrounded by protective plexiglass.

“This is the biggest match in club football,” explained Joel Jaggar, a British transplant who’s lived in Barcelona for a decade. Jaggar runs Real Vintage Football, a rare jersey boutique in El Raval (just outside of the city’s Gothic Quarter) that was unsurprisingly overwhelmed with visitors all weekend. He’d stocked up on at least a hundred Messi jerseys, all of which were gone by the time they closed on Friday. “A lot of people come to Barcelona specifically for this game,” he shared. “Big fans, sure, but also people who want to experience the game for the sake of the experience.” Globally, he argued, the word “Barcelona” is as closely associated with the club as it is the city. “You can’t say the same for Madrid,” he quipped.

Jaggar and I met the morning after a swell of student-organized protests regarding the city’s cost-of-living crisis boiled over into vandalism and looting near the neighborhood that houses his store. There was even suspicion El Clásico would have to be postponed or cancelled altogether. Come morning though, those anxieties had faded–as is so often the case, football continued uninterrupted.

Even with capacity capped at around 62,000 during ongoing renovations, Camp Nou is something to behold. Ahead of kickoff, supporters lifted color-coordinated pieces of poster nestled into each seat,forming a massive, stadium-wide tifo in the colors of the club and, in our section, the Catalonian flag. Fireworks exploded, and El Clásico began.

But much of the match’s first half was mired in tension, as Barcelona dominated possession but failed to score. The crowd finally began to pop when Madrid nabbed their first goal, then properly exploded when Barca tied it up in the second half. When Mbappé scored his second goal of the evening at ‘87 minutes after a beautiful assist from Bellingham, he vaulted over to the gate of the Barcelona supporters’ section, taunting them as trash thrown from the crowd rained over him. Madrid held on, gaining the tiniest of advantages in the El Clásico win count: 107 to their rivals’ 106.

As we walked out of Camp Nou, which will hold a whopping 105,000 spectators when its renovation is complete, I thought about the sort of sporting events that tend to grace bucket lists: Super Bowls and World Cup Finals and World Series games. So often the point is the spectacle of a season or tournament ending—the big trophy, the championship ring, the confetti and fireworks. El Clásico is different. There’s no trophy at the end, no ultimate resolution. Like the ideological divide for which it’s become a proxy, El Clásico is in a constant state of ebb and flow. Each time Barcelona and Real Madrid take the pitch, there’s no decisive victory so much as another brick laid in the foundation of football’s greatest rivalry. These clubs have come to blows 265 times. But as long as there are football stadiums in Barcelona and Madrid, El Clásico will endure as a conduit for the enduring rancor between the country's two biggest cities.

#6732. Only the Very Best Gifts for Mom

There are two types of moms. There’s the one who immediately tries on the lipstick, lights the candle, and slips into her new sweater, and the one who saves your present, in its original box, for when the time is right. You love her for who she is, even when her quirks drive you mad. One thing’s for certain, though: When the holidays roll around, she’s thinking about everyone but herself.

So what to get the mother figure who puts everyone else first? For new mom Andrea Zendejas, the best gifts are those that fill gaps—usually in her wardrobe or her beauty routine. For lifestyle editor Elly Leavitt, it’s knowing what will actually get used: “I’ve learned over the years what materials and colors she likes, and which she resolutely does not (polyester is public enemy number one). My mom is a chef and a recipe developer, so when in doubt, a beautiful kitchen tool that doubles as a prop is the most foolproof approach.”

A handwritten note and a bouquet of her favorite flowers are where you start; our guide is where you find the accompanying perfect gift. Read on for our edit of gifts for moms they’ll love—even if they’d never ask.

She wants for nothing and needs even less. The MO to follow here is “Don’t overdo it.” Gift her something that feels like a discovery, such as a candle with a transportive scent (Flamingo Estate’s Sappho Fig is my kind of heaven), nourishing hand creams to defend against winter dryness, or luxurious socks she wouldn’t pick up for herself.

Usually sold out but restocked just for the holidays, Flamingo Estate’s fig-scented duo will put her right back on the Mediterranean—mentally, at least.

You’ve graduated from gifting reed sticks (amateur) to scented terracotta (professional). Brava!

The kind of thing Mom would appropriate from you in a heartbeat if she clocked it in your bag. Here’s to you anticipating her moves.

Only the best for Mom, head to toe, from one of our favorite brands.

A cozy winter essential, little explanation necessary.

A trio of scented balms for the mom who always seems to have a hand cream in every coat pocket.

Bohemian or artsy, she is not; she wants something classic and high quality. Minimize the margin of error and go for something that’ll last, like a decadent silk pajama set, a pair of shearling-lined slippers, or one of Mémoire’s leather-bound notebooks—they come in plain and embossed leathers and can be monogrammed.

The one that got away… or so she thought. Chanel’s coveted animal-print heels have returned for the season, and we have it on good authority she’ll love these.

For recording her to-dos and recipes, send her one of Mémoire’s refillable notebooks. It arrives in an elegant teal gift box with lovely ribbon; I happen to personally love the smaller size in this croc-embossed leather.

If you’re thinking about gifting her silk pajamas, Olivia von Halle’s divine sets are probably already on your mind.

These and one of Bellocq’s beautifully packaged teas, and your job is done.

Burberry’s iconic checked scarf in a festive red—get it embroidered with her initials for a personal touch.

Extra comfort for her Sunday walks. Size up by one, as these run a little snug due to the shearling lining.

She touts the benefits of deep diaphragmatic breathing and evangelizes the virtues of ice-cold showers in the morning; she’ll share her entire closet with you, but won’t let you near her favorite serum. A beauty gift that fits seamlessly into her curated routine is exactly what she wants expects.

One of the best LED face masks on the market. We know because we’ve tested it.

La Mer’s newest concentrated serum, formulated for stressed, irritated skin; it’s also excellent for post-treatment maintenance.

A travel-size hair-care set of my personal go-tos. I’ve given these to my mom before and will definitely give them to her again.

For the sake of transparency…I may be gifting the hand cream to my mom, but I’m keeping the eye patches for myself. Sharing is caring, right?

Can confirm: She’s not thinking about getting herself a new hairbrush. Which means she’ll be pleasantly surprised when she receives this one.

She keeps a bottle of Dom Pérignon chilled in the fridge and refuses to serve a meal without setting a pretty table. What’s a more perfect gift for the consummate host than something to elevate her next kitchen exploits?

There will be no harsh overhead lighting at her dinner parties.

#6731. Bikini Trimmers Are Painless, Easy, and Irritation-Free—if You Know Which Ones to Buy

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

“Bush on main,” “hot bush summer,” “don’t shave your bush!”—I see it all over TikTok and Instagram, and I’m all for this pro-pubes movement we’re in. But I haven’t exactly seen it anywhere, and none of my friends seem to be participating. In fact, I get more questions than ever about the best razors and shaving products. What I do think is happening, though, is a transition to trimming rather than a full Brazilian bikini wax. Which is where my endorsement (er, some might say campaign) for bikini trimmers comes in.

I ditched all the other at-home hair removal methods for a bikini trimmer in 2023, aside from the occasional wax or laser-hair removal device. Truly, nothing is as quick, easy, or painless as trimming my bikini line with an electric razor. Because I’ve reviewed so many bikini trimmers over the years, I’ve learned that the best one really depends on what you’re trying to do. My everyday electric bikini trimmer, my travel-friendly option, and my precision trimmer all serve different purposes—and each one does its specific job a little better than the rest. Here’s a peek at my faves:

Don’t worry, I’m getting into all the best bikini trimmers I’ve tried below. And if you need even more info to help you choose, keep reading for expert advice on how to trim your bikini line and which trimmer is best for you.

Why we love it: When I traveled to Miami to celebrate my birthday, this bikini trimmer from Bushbalm was one of the first things I threw in my travel bag. Why? Because I was able to charge it before my trip, then never had to worry about it dying on me in the middle of a pre-pool trim, thanks to the battery that lasts a full five hours. (TBH, I actually didn’t even charge it before—I’ve actually only needed to charge this once in the last six months, lol.)

And while I could wax poetic about the battery forever, I mostly love how powerful this little trimmer is while still feeling gentle against my skin. I rarely use it with the guard on, but I love having it as an option when I want a slightly longer length between waxing appointments. The trimmer gets pretty much as close to the skin as you can get without causing nicks and is so easy to use that I could do it with my eyes closed. It does the job and does it well, and I love that it’s waterproof in case you want to use it in the bath or shower. I’m also obsessed with the ingrown hair oil from BushBalm—it makes the perfect pairing.”—HANNAH CHUBB, FORMER LIFESTYLE EDITOR

If there’s any straggling hairs after you shave, this is a great extra tool to have on hand for getting them. But don’t worry if you’re clumsy (like us), the blade isn’t too sharp. This waterproof trimmer is great for in the shower too, thanks to the lightweight handle.

Why we love it: IDK if a day passes when I don't see this trimmer on my FYP. I’ve seen a gazillion videos reviewing this, calling it the best hair-removal device for way, way, way cheaper than laser or wax passes. It’s double-ended: One side is a classic blade trimmer that takes off the hair, while the other is a precision trimmer that you run along your skin in circular motions to get the closest possible trim.

One Cosmo editor says: “Having the two options for trimming really helps get my skin smooth and reduces any stubble whatsoever. It’s also a huge plus that this device is super easy to use.”—BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: If you usually end up with nicks in your bikini area because razors or trimmers aren’t contoured to your body, you’ll love the V-shape of this bikini trimmer, which easily trims all the lil folds and crevices of your bikini line. Not to mention, the three comb attachments give you choices for how much hair you want to remove in each area. And don’t worry about cuts, because the rounded blade glides across your skin without getting caught.

One Cosmo editor says: “I use this electric razor to trim my dad’s neck hair (sorry for blowing up your spot, Dad). But!! With that said, it proves just how simple this is to use. My dad would certainly screech in pain if I even remotely knicked him, and I never have with this (others, not so confident).”—BG

Why we love it: My battle with ingrown hairs is essentially what led me to try a bikini trimmer in the first place. And the first one on my list was this tool from Meridian, which went viral on TikTok because it’s got a strong, sharp blade that cuts through super-thick and curly hair without causing irritation (i.e., ingrown hairs and razor burn). I usually pop on one of the two included trim guards, which keeps the length a lil longer but helps further prevent inflamed bumps.

I’ll just exfoliate in the shower, rinse, then lather up a moisturizing, fragrance-free shaving cream and run the trimmer along my bikini line. I also make sure to avoid going over each area too many times to reduce irritation. Afterward, I’ll pat my skin dry and smooth on a gentle body lotion to keep my skin soft and smooth.

One Cosmo editor says: "Of all of the bikini trimmers I've tested—which is a lot, btw—this is definitely the most gentle. I've never, ever worried that I would nick myself, but it's been especially helpful for cutting down on ingrown hairs."—BG

Why we love it: One of the most necessary situations for a quality bikini trimmer is while traveling to quickly clean up any unwanted hairs without pain or irritation. Enter: This travel, touch-up trimmer from Bushbalm that won a Cosmo Holy Grail Beauty award. The stainless steel blades help pick up teeny-tiny hairs (including even peach fuzz on your face) in only a few minutes. But the best part is that it really doesn’t nick or irritate skin—a must for quick use.

One Cosmo editor says: “I love how easy it is to quickly touch up my bikini line with this, and it’s entirely painless. Best part: It’s waterproof, so I can use it in the shower to save time.”—BG
