Say you want a super smooth, hair-free bikini line but just wanna trim everything else? Run the trimmer along your skin, then pop on the razor head to get a close shave. This tool is also really helpful on the precision front, thanks to an LED light on the head of the device that makes it way easier to get in all those little hard-to-reach crevices that you can't see super well.

One Cosmo editor says: “I’ve used this tool for years, but I really fell in love when they relaunched this year. Yes, it’s expensive, but it feels fancy. The tool is weighted, but not too heavy, and made of a sturdy plastic that feels really good in my hand. And it also looks nice in my bathroom. I just keep it in my shower and charge up the pedestal once a week.”—BG

Why we love it: Just because my bikini line is covered in razor bumps after a less-than-stellar shave doesn’t mean I’m fine with the overgrowth. In those moments, I reach for this gentle Gillette Venus trimmer, which comes with a removable guard that helps me lightly trim my hair without irritation. “Using a comb attachment with a trimmer can prevent the blades from touching the skin at all, which helps avoid irritation and nicks and cuts,” says Katelyn Liston, associate scientist for Gillette Venus.

One Cosmo editor says: "When I found myself suddenly en route to pool party in Miami with a bikini line I hadn't touched in months, I ran over to Target and snatched this up in a pinch. Shockingly, it was so freaking easy to use. The guard helps reduce the potential for cuts and bumps, so I could quickly clean up all the stray hairs without worrying it would irritate me."—BG

No, a bikini trimmer is not necessarily better than shaving; it just comes down to personal preference and the look you're going for. “You should consider a trimmer if you experience irritation after shaving your bikini area, or if you prefer to maintain, but not fully remove, all of your pubic hair,” says Liston. Shaving, on the other hand, cuts the hair off at the surface, meaning you’re left with smoother skin afterward—but also with a higher risk of bumps, ingrown hairs, and irritation.

Do you need to use shaving cream with a bikini trimmer?

Yes, you can use shaving cream with a bikini trimmer if you want more moisturized skin, but you don’t have to. You can also trim your hair dry without any cream or lotion, which is why bikini trimmers are so versatile and easy to use. Check the instructions on your specific trimmer though, as some with extra-sharp blades might require shaving cream to help prevent friction that can lead to irritation.

Bikini trimmers do leave some stubble because your hair isn’t actually removed from your follicle like with plucking or waxing, or cut close to the skin like with shaving. Some trimmers have a strong, sharp blade that can get really close to your skin, leaving you with little to no noticeable hair, while others (generally best for sensitive skin) just trim your hair to a shorter length. If you prefer a totally hair-free finish, you can shave or wax and then just use a bikini trimmer to clean up any hairs as they’re starting to grow back.

A couple of days before you want to trim your bikini line, exfoliate your skin with a chemical exfoliant or body scrub to get a smoother shave, advises board-certified dermatologist Mona Gohara. Then, once you’re ready, lather up your skin with a hydrating, fragrance-free shaving cream or oil to avoid irritation and bumps when shaving or using a trimmer in the shower (you don't need cream when you're trimming on dry skin though, fyi).

The easiest way to prevent bumps and ingrown hair when trimming your bikini line? Make sure you’re shaving with the grain (i.e., the direction your hair grows), says Dr. Gohara, because shaving and trimming against the grain (or switching up your direction) is basically a recipe for irritation. “Take it slow and don’t rush,” she says.

Every bikini trimmer is different. “Using your trimmer incorrectly can cause discomfort, or even worse, cut your sensitive bikini-line skin,” says Liston. Some trimmers require that you use shaving cream, others need to be used dry; so, make sure you’re following the directions on the packaging of your trimmer (aka read it before tossing it out).

As soon as you’re done trimming, follow up with a generous layer of fragrance-free cream safe to use on your sensitive, external skin to keep your skin soft and bump-free, says Dr. Gohara. P.S., It's never a bad idea to dab on an ingrown hair toner while maintaining your bikini line—just dab one on every few days post cleansing.

Shaving or trimming with a dull, dirty blade is not ideal and can often lead to irritation or infections. Clean your blades with antibacterial soap and water after every single use, and make sure you're replacing your blades every few weeks for a more painless experience (you can even put a reminder on your calendar!). Also, never share your tools—even if your roommate asks nicely.

How do I prevent ingrown hairs when using a bikini trimmer?

Bikini trimmers don’t generally cause ingrown hairs because the hair isn’t removed from your skin, which means it's unlikely to get trapped as it grows. This is why bikini trimmers are a great option for anyone who’s prone to ingrown hairs, razor bumps, and razor burn.

Though a bikini trimmer is less likely to give you ingrown hairs, adhering to the following practices will help reduce your risk:

History and hair type: If your skin is sensitive, you have a history of ingrowns, or you have curly or coarse hair, your chance of developing ingrown hairs increases. Though there's nothing you can do about this, if you follow the tips below, your chances can significantly decrease.

Preparation: You should ideally wait until your pubic hair has grown back out to around 4mm, as any shorter can lead to irritation. You also need to exfoliate beforehand, and if your trimmer/razor works with it, add shaving cream or oil.

Direction of shave: Despite razors or trimmers claiming you can shave whichever way and not have any irritation, it truly is best to shave in the direction of your hair growth using short, small strokes.

Aftercare: Trimming is a form of exfoliation, so natural oils and moisture may be stripped away during the process. Layering on a post-shave moisturizer (that's fragrance-free and is applied only on your external genitalia area!) will provide moisture and soothe your skin.

Blade: Surprisingly enough, you should be changing your blades after five to 10 shaves. A dull blade can lead to more nicks and can increase bacteria buildup, which is a recipe for infection.

And, the most important rule: If you have a significant amount of ingrown hairs (or even just one!), let them heal and prevent picking. If you try to remove them yourself, you can risk infection.

Are you determined to get rid of razor burn or ingrown hairs? A trimmer is probably the better option. A bikini trimmer is also great if you don’t want to completely get rid of hair in an area. Need a completely smooth shave? You’ll probably want to shave or wax first, then use a trimmer to touch up between.

If you want to create precise designs or just touch-up your bikini line between shaving, you’ll like having a smaller bikini trimmer that can easily maneuver across your skin. However, if you like trimming your lower stomach, inner thighs, or other larger areas, opt for something that has a bigger blade (and if it comes with a bikini attachment for precision-work, even better).

Some trimmers are a little bit more ~advanced.~ Choose a trimmer that’s waterproof if you want to save time and trim in the shower. If you want a super-close shave, look for a razor/trimmer duo, or an electric razor that has a trimmer attachment.

If you trim, like, all the time, grab one that comes with replacement heads to make sure you’ll always have a sharp blade. I also live for a trimmer that has a light to help me see what the heck I’m doing down there (because there is nothing worse than going in there blind with a sharp object, right?).

#6728. Prince William and George Go on a Fun Father-Son Date to Barcelona

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The duo cheered on their favorite football club, Aston Villa, at a UEFA Champions League match

Prince William and Prince George love a football match. The royal father-son duo has made it somewhat of a tradition to travel together to cheer on their favorite football club, Aston Villa. Last April, they went from Paris to Birmingham, England, within days to watch their players in the quarter finals. And yesterday, they watched Aston Villa go up against FC Barcelona at the Spanish team’s home base, Spotify Camp Nou in Barcelona.

The royals coordinated in cool, easy game-day looks for the UEFA Champions League match. William traded his go-to navy suit for a navy windbreaker and a black baseball cap. Meanwhile, his eldest son looked so grown up in a black Aston Villa x Adidas track jacket.

Growing up, most of William’s friends supported powerhouse football clubs like Manchester United or Chelsea, but the royal has said he chose to put his faith on a smaller, less popular team—and Aston Villa won him over early. Now, the Prince of Wales has passed his allegiance to his children, particularly 13-year-old George, who has been going to football matches and big sporting events with his parents since he was a little kid (see this throwback from 2021).

“I’m kind of hoping they’ll all find their own teams in time,” William once said. “They don’t all have to be Villa fans. I’m trying not to be biased, but obviously they see how passionate I am about it and they watch the matches with me.”

#6727. The Luxury Fashion Police Are Coming for the Makeup-Bags-as-a-Purse Girls. But They Should Back Off

Anything can be a bag if you really want it to be and the internet outrage around this says more about elitism than style.

There is a particular breed of luxury fashion enthusiast who is personally offended by the idea of a woman taking an expensive bag that is not technically designed to be a purse and, well, using it as a purse. The latest offenders? Anyone turning Hermès Bride-a-Brac pouches and Bolide cosmetic cases, or Chanel makeup bags into their go-to everyday handbag. To these particular fashion enthusiasts, doing such a thing is basically committing a fashion felony.

The argument is that these products are not real handbags. That wearing a cosmetic case on your shoulder is “tacky” and that it cheapens the brand as a whole (presumably for those who can afford the usually even pricier actual bags). But why does it matter?

The Hermès Bride-a-Brac, for example, was originally designed as a wash bag or travel pouch. It has a structured canvas body, a wide-opening zippered top, and it is perfectly suited to hold all the things women actually carry around with them: A phone, cardholder, sunglasses, keys, lip products, and whatever other miscellaneous objects that accumulate in the bottom of a purse.

There is also something particularly funny about the outrage of women repurposing these bags when you consider the history of luxury handbags themselves. The Hermès Evelyne began as a grooming bag for horse equipment, designed with perforations to allow brushes to dry. The Louis Vuitton Noé was originally created to carry bottles of champagne. These are now some of the most recognizable luxury bags in the world.

Even the mythology surrounding the Birkin has always been rooted in the idea of a bag that is meant to be lived in. Jane Birkin largely saw the staple piece as an everyday work bag meant to be heavily used and filled to the brim. The original vision was not necessarily some precious object to be locked away in a dust bag and admired from across the bedroom. So it actually makes perfect sense for people to use their makeup bags as purses, if they so please.

This idea that people shouldn’t do this because it’s “tacky” quickly reveals the class politics hiding underneath so much of the fashion industry. Luxury has become wrapped up in exclusivity that people have started treating the products themselves as elitist identifiers. Owning the right bag, wearing the right logo, and knowing the unwritten rules of fashion are all supposedly proof that you belong to a certain world. And when someone finds a creative way to participate without following the exact spending requirements, it seems to make certain people very uncomfortable.

I will never forget sitting at a large dinner table when another fashion editor asked me when I was finally going to invest in a “real designer bag,” suggesting that my $400 Coach purse simply wasn't good enough. I remember asking her, quite sincerely, whether she planned on buying it for me if it was such a pressing concern. She didn’t have an answer, but I’ll never forget how it made me feel. That fashion and personal style has a price tag we all aren’t meant to meet.

You can love Hermès and still think a Bride-a-Brac makes a great everyday bag. You can appreciate Chanel's history and still think a cosmetic pouch looks adorable with a chain strap. These ideas are not mutually exclusive.

Women have been repurposing pieces in their closets forever. We have quite literally always done things like turn belts into necklaces, oversized shirts into dresses, and whatever else happens to be lying around into something worth wearing. Fashion and improvisation go hand in hand. Some of the most interesting personal style I’ve seen comes from people who aren't particularly concerned with whether an item was originally intended to be worn a certain way. A bag is a bag if it holds your things and you like carrying it. People are making this much more complicated than it needs to be.

Moreover, I also don't believe in pocket-watching. Not everyone has thousands of dollars to spend on a handbag, and they shouldn't feel obligated to. If someone's luxury budget allows for a Chanel makeup bag rather than a classic flap bag, and they decide to put a strap on it and carry it to dinner, where exactly is the problem? If anything, I find it entertaining to see someone get creative with what they already own.

At the end of the day, there are so many bigger things going on in the world. None of this is that serious. A makeup bag can be a purse. A scarf can be a belt. A skirt can be a top. And a woman does not need the approval of someone else to wear what she wants how she wants to wear it.

Aiyana Ishmael is the style editor at Cosmopolitan magazine. In her work, Aiyana focuses on the culture of fashion and how it intertwines and shapes the zeitgeist. She is an award-winning journalist from Miami, Florida, and a graduate of the historically Black university, Florida A&M. She is a 2024 Forbes 30 Under 30: Media honoree, and her debut romance novella PASSING GAME is set to release March of 2027 (831 Stories/Simon & Schuster).

#6725. A Covid-19 Meme Inspired Alex Prager’s First Movie, DreamQuil

The Californian artist talks to Bazaar about her feature debut

A lex Prager, known for her theatrical photography, stumbled upon “YearQuil,” a fake twist on NyQuil that lets you “sleep until 2021.” This doomscroll discovery stuck with Prager and her sister, Vanessa, also an artist, who then spent the rest of the Covid lockdown writing a screenplay based on that idea. The two imagined a device designed to help an unhappy woman escape everyday life, and the result is her feature-film debut, DreamQuil.

“I really wanted to direct movies,” Prager tells Bazaar on a video call from her Los Angeles home. “We just dove into this idea of a woman deciding to be willingly put to sleep so she doesn’t have to experience the uncomfortable sides of life.… We loved the idea of taking the easy way and the problems that come with that, and this cautionary tale.”

Prager, now 47, dropped out of high school and learned photography without formal training. She turned her camera on people living in her hometown neighborhood, Los Feliz. The artist channeled her love for film, particularly the golden age of Hollywood and film noir, into sweeping mise-en-scène photographs imbued with a distinctly California sensibility. Some images feature tender close-ups with kaleidoscopic color palettes; others capture hyperpopulated streets littered with uncanny and surreal treasures. She soon began exhibiting her work at galleries worldwide, and she’s currently preparing for a solo show at Lehmann Maupin in New York for March 2027.

This command over composition and color carries directly into her film work. The artist’s short films like Despair (2010), starring Bryce Dallas Howard, and Uncanny Valley (2018), starring Cate Blanchett, show Prager’s deft understanding of the screen and her ability to stage illusory, suspenseful scenes that convey emotion to the audience. This hypersensory storytelling technique fuels DreamQuil’s 84-minute run time, which Prager meticulously composed. “I storyboarded every frame of the movie before I even brought my cinematographer on,” she says, adding that she drew visual inspiration from Douglas Sirk melodramas and Technicolor classics, including a lush field of poppies referencing The Wizard of Oz. Each scene pops with Technicolor palettes, but beyond the decadent surface, Prager shows how color in her work is intentionally symbolic.

“Color can communicate so much,” Prager says. “The red [of Carol’s dress] was intended as symbolic for her humanity—blood red. Then the cold blue [dress] of Carol Two that she wears is representative of these tech companies and technology really, and how it’s unfeeling—the opposite of being human.”

The luscious color palette in her photography and cinematic work often means to “soften the blow” and invite people into difficult conversations about the state of the world. “I’ve always done that with my photography,” she says. “It’s been a way to talk about things that are really challenging for me or things that are going on in the world that are hard to comprehend because they’re so dark and terrifying. It brings color and beauty; it adds a layer of delight and softness, and underneath you can unpack the layers depending on who you are and where you’re at in your life.”

The issues she examines in DreamQuil are indeed thorny. Like The Stepford Wives (2004), Carol’s story wrestles with familiar themes: the demands of femininity and marriage and control in a patriarchal society. But the Prager sisters narrowed in on a more incisive critique of corporatism and AI. The advertisements, in particular, visually assault Carol with tailor-made pleas from businesses preying on her insecurities. “It seemed like this idea of being very far in the future—now, it seems like it’s happening already,” she says. At every juncture, the villain stems from what lies behind the callous Carol Two robot, a sinister system creeping up to steal our humanity.

Prager fixates on our shared humanity in everything she does. She often examines the thin line between artificiality and reality through saturated, populous portraits. The themes coursing through her body of work all coalesce in DreamQuil, a critique of the changing world around us. Her next film is more personal. “I grew up in Los Angeles. I was born and raised in Los Feliz, and I shoot everything within around a 10-block radius of there,” she says. “This will be a sinister love letter to Los Angeles.”

#6724. What Writing a Book About JFK Jr. Taught Me About Personal Style

John F. Kennedy Jr. wasn’t the first guy to ride a bike wearing a suit. Likewise, the presidential scion and People’s 1988 Sexiest Man Alive didn’t invent the backward Kangol cap, the pinstriped suit, or wearing a waistcoat with jeans—he just did it better than pretty much anybody else. That’s a big part of why he was one of the most photographed men on the planet in the 1990s, and why he remains a menswear moodboard staple today, nearly 30 years after his untimely death. Still, no matter how hard you try, and no matter how closely you hew to the source material, all attempts to replicate JFK Jr.’s style are destined to leave you looking like a runner-up in a Washington Square Park lookalike contest.

Ask anyone who encountered John-John in his prime—which, in my experience, is virtually everyone who lived in Manhattan in the 1990s—and the first thing they’ll mention is his magnetism, his charm, and the fact that, against all odds, he was actually better-looking in person. I know this because, having recently written a book about JFK Jr.’s style, I’ve spent a good part of the last year poring over photos, reading biographies, speaking to people who knew him, and otherwise immersing myself in his world. Among the conclusions I’ve come to is that you probably won’t look as cool as JFK Jr. did wearing rollerblades, but there are a few style moves you can borrow from his playbook without resorting to full Kennedy cosplay.

JFK Jr.’s wardrobe was built on four pillars: tailoring, preppy basics, workwear, and sportswear. But it was the idiosyncratic way he combined them (i.e., a backward baseball cap with a suit, a polo neck sweater under a tweed blazer, hiking boots with tailored trousers) that resulted in some of his most memorable looks. Surprisingly, while the combination of straight-leg jeans, a button-up dress shirt, and a blazer has become shorthand for JFK Jr.’s style, I’ve never seen any evidence of him wearing it. In fact, he seldom wore jeans at all (some speculate his mother thought they looked shabby, while others told me that jeans didn’t complement his active lifestyle). That said, plenty of other ’90s style icons, from Ralph Lauren to Jerry Seinfeld, rocked this particular look, and it’s a pretty easy one to pull off. As with everything about JFK Jr.’s style, it’s not about lifting these combinations wholesale, but understanding the precise balance of contrast that makes them work.

One of the most consistent elements of JFK Jr.’s style was his love of discordant accessories, from his collection of flat caps to his ever-present wallet chain to his Oakley Eye Jacket shades. Some of these were practical—he was so prone to misplacing his wallet and keys that he needed to chain them to his pants—but others reflected his love of color (funky knit caps), his enthusiasm for skiing (fleece headbands), and his passion for travel (various hats picked up on trips to India and Thailand). Some accessories will suit you better than others, but if you have a personal connection to it, all the better. And most importantly, limit yourself to one standout add-on per outfit.

Speaking of accessories: JFK Jr. was a dedicated scarf guy, and he’d pair one with everything from joggers and sneakers to his best navy blue suit. Rather than draping a scarf loosely around his neck, he tended to tie it in either a dressy overhand or classic Parisian knot, both of which lend a more formal look. He also loved wearing a thick scarf as outerwear, throwing it over a sweatshirt on a chilly day instead of a coat, often paired with a knit cap. For best results, don’t pick a scarf that matches the rest of your outfit too closely.

Among the most surprising things I discovered about JFK Jr.’s wardrobe was his watch collection. I wasn’t able to uncover any evidence of him wearing the long-lost Rolex Daytona given to him by Aristotle Onassis (a watch that, if ever found, would give Paul Newman a run for his money), but I did identify an unusually wide range of other pieces in his collection, from a Victorinox Swiss Army field watch to a Swatch chronograph to a vintage Rolex Oyster Perpetual. This is of a piece with everything else we know about JFK Jr.: he wasn’t bound by any single style, brand, or price point, and got just as much enjoyment out of a cheap quartz watch as a luxury mechanical one.

As a rich kid raised on the Upper East Side, JFK Jr.’s closet included plenty of blazers, chinos, and polos. He was a gym rat who loved playing sports, so he also had a lot of sweats, tees, and sneakers. He was a downtown socialite, so he owned a lot of nice suits and tuxes, too. He also had an enviable collection of graphic tees from NYC institutions like the Russian & Turkish Baths in the East Village and the New York Athletic Club, as well as some commemorating events like the 1984 NYC Fresh Festival, a seminal moment in hip-hop history. What stands out to me—and what I believe makes his style so distinctive—is that JFK Jr.’s wardrobe was an authentic reflection of who he was, where he came from, and what he loved. This might be the most important style lesson any of us can learn from him: Great personal style is less about clothes than wearing them in a way that expresses your individuality.

American Style: The Look and Legacy of JFK Jr. is now available for pre-order.

#6722. How to Get Chocolate Chrome Nails, Hailey Bieber’s Go-To Fall Manicure

Brown polish and chrome powder will never go out of style.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

In the unending trend cycle, there’s one fall manicure that has been trending every year for almost half a decade: chocolate chrome. “I think the secret behind the popularity of chocolate brown shades with chrome powder is how they give a familiar color palette a completely new look,” Los Angeles-based nail artist Vikki Swan says. “Chrome creates a beautiful wet-look effect with delicate shine and soft reflections.”

Our Lady of Unending Nail Inspiration, Hailey Bieber, has been a fan of brown chrome nails for years: She went with more of an iced latte shade in September 2022 (the same month she wore the cooler taupe version in the photo below) and I found evidence of her wearing a deeper chocolate glaze in 2022, 2023, 2024, 2025, and, most recently, about a month ago. Bieber is known for her clean-girl aesthetic, which brown chrome definitely can fit into, but when done with a dark base polish the look can feel almost edgy—more glam rock than soft girl.

That’s all to say, chocolate chrome nails are versatile, with about a million variations that look great on every nail length and skin tone. Here, some expert tips for getting the look yourself, plus a handful of inspiration photos to bring to the salon.

“Chocolate chrome” follows the time-honored tradition of naming beauty looks after food, but these manicures are simply brown nail polish (usually gel) with chrome powder on top. Still, that basic combination can lead to some very decadent final results. “Chocolate chrome takes the richness of espresso and transforms it into the shine of molten jewelry,” says Tucson-based nail artist Max Estrada. “It's glamorous, seductive and beautifully indulgent.”

Estrada sees this year’s chocolate chromes as emblematic of a larger beauty trend. “I'm seeing a move away from apologetically understated manicures toward luxury that feels almost theatrical,” Estrada says. “Think New York or Paris in 1987 with extravagant faux-fur coats, vintage Schiaparelli, opulent dresses and a cloud of Dior Poison.”

With so many shades of brown and so many chrome powders to choose from, picking one for your own chocolate manicure can feel overwhelming. The first step is deciding what vibe you want. “Darker browns create a richer, more dramatic effect, while lighter shades give a softer, more delicate finish,” Swan says, noting that she generally prefers browns with a neutral or slightly warm undertone for this.

Whether you go with a nearly-black cocoa or a creamy caramel as your base, Estrada recommends chromes that lean more gold than silver: Think champagne, bronze, or copper (though “an airy pearl chrome adds a cooler editorial twist”). Regardless of which color you prefer, Swan says to look for a sheer chrome with a delicate pearlescent finish. “They enhance the depth of the base color without covering or overpowering the original gel polish shade,” she explains.

Brown chrome also works well in a mismatched manicure, another example of nails trending toward the theatrical. “Let's be extravagant!” Estrada says. “Mix chocolate chrome with black-cherry velvet cat-eye, deep oxblood French tips, tortoiseshell, baroque gold detailing, sculpted accents and tiny crystals.” Swan also loves brown chrome paired with tortoiseshell and gold. “The key is to stay within a cohesive color palette so the different designs complement each other without making the manicure look too busy,” she says.

While it is possible to do chrome over regular polish, the look is more commonly created with a gel set, since the base color needs to be totally dry before you rub the chrome on top. “I recommend applying chrome powder after curing, while the top coat is still warm,” Swan says. “After application, it’s important to seal the chrome thoroughly with a finishing top coat to minimize the risk of chipping and ensure long-lasting results.”

Both Swan also emphasizes the importance of having a smooth base under the chrome, which requires thorough nail preparation. “Chrome powder can highlight even the smallest imperfections,” she says. “Proper nail architecture and proportions are essential, along with a high-quality top coat that creates a perfectly smooth surface.”

While chocolate chrome can work on any nail length, Estrada thinks they’re especially luxurious on longer tips. “I love these colors on a long structured coffin, exaggerated almond or beautifully sharp stiletto,” he says, noting that long nails make the decadent finish even more striking. “This winter, I want excess, glamour and artistry. Too much is precisely the point!”

Kara McGrath is a contributing beauty editor at Cosmopolitan with over 15 years of experience covering the beauty space and an at-home nail art enthusiast. Both jobs include browsing and saving countless manicure ideas for research, plus chatting with professional artists for insight into current nail trends.

Kara McGrath is a writer and editor with over 17 years of experience covering the beauty industry, women’s health, and a wide range of other lifestyle and wellness topics. After most recently serving as content director at Allure (2019-2025) and executive editor at Bustle (2014-2019), she is now fully freelance. In addition to Cosmo, her writing has appeared in Allure, The New York Times, Marie Claire, Business of Fashion, Playboy, NH Magazine, and more. Kara lives in New Hampshire with her dog, four cats, and a rotating cast of foster animals. You can find more of her work at karamcgrath.com.
